4. Advantages and disadvantages of shading by photography
This photographic shading process may be capable of further development. In most cases at
present it is unable to satisfy the high demands made upon it. Because of the relatively
complicated apparatus it is economical only when much work is to be carried out, and only
if the models to be constructed can serve other purposes than the production of one map,
or again if the model is constructed to be used as such and the photography is a by-
product of this procedure. Normally the models are not sufficiently accurate. So far they
have been produced at the scale of the map to be shaded, having been designed to suit the
relief image of this particular map. It is, however, a truism that one can never model with the
accuracy and detail with which one can draw, since the material just does not permit it. All
models, produced mechanically, are quite inadequate in their portrayal of small rugged rock
formations, steep steps, karst formations, etc. Instead they show flattened-out profiles in
areas of marked ridge lines. In cartography, on the one hand, great stress is placed on the
trueness of scale on scribed sheets, while on the other there is a blithe acceptance of models
made for maps whose plaster forms far from satisfy these accuracy demands.
In large and medium scale topographic maps the shaded image should be everywhere
within 0.2 mm of contour bends and angles, rocky ridges, moraine crests etc. For the
reasons mentioned, however, this cannot be achieved without thorough and intensive
Further discrepancies between the map image and the hill shading produced photo-
graphically may often develop from the differences in projection, mentioned above, for
which there may not have been complete compensation.
Another failing of ‘photographic’ shading is the poor adaptation of light direction to the
land forms. As was discussed in more detail above, parallel light gives rise to numerous
‘lifeless’ or ‘expressionless’ areas in the relief. Often photographs of relief models may
appear extremely impressive to the casual observer, but closer examination would reveal the
arbitrary quality of the shaded forms. The impressions of images may not always be the
There is another even greater failing. Mechanically produced models and their lighting
effects do not take account of the generalization of relief forms, the increase or reduction of
contrast, often required in the map image. Photographic shading suffers most of all from a
chaotic confusion of individual forms. To overcome this disadvantage the model and even
the base map contours themselves would have to be generalized to the specifications of the
required shading image, and this would have to be done for each scale group. This proce-
dure, however, would be much more laborious, much more time-consuming and difficult
than the drawing and generalization of shading done by hand; and even with these efforts
the result would not match the quality of the dra
Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF' - H. Model photography (p.205-208): "Mechanically produced models and their lighting effects do not take accoun
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF'

- H. Model photography (p.205-208): "Mechanically produced models and their lighting effects do not take
  account of the generalization ... Photographic shading suffers most of all from a chaotic confusion of
  individual forms." Parallel light -> expressionless areas. Hand shading within 0.2 mm of contour bends.
- J. COMPUTER SHADING (p.209-212) Yoeli (his student), Brassel, Huegli: cosine of light vs facet normal.
  Imhof's three difficulties: (1) parallel light makes acute forms vanish and obtuse ones look acute;
  cure is "extremely delicate adaptation of the assumed light direction to the local topographical
  requirements ... probably beyond the formulation of a programming logic"; (2) simultaneous contrast:
  "a flat area interrupting a dark slope ... will appear too bright ... could be interpreted as a rising
  slope"; the cartographer corrects it; (3) generalization: "The computer program is 'blind'";
  "a cartographic relief at small scales is not just a geometrical reduction of the natural forms but
  the creation of a new relief depiction". His suggestions: (A) print a second shading with the light
  turned 10-15 deg and combine them; (B) treat the computed shading as a working base to be retouched
  by hand (local darkening/lightening of individual forms).
  -> THIS IS MY PROGRAM for code: (1) local light adaptation from the landform's own orientation,
  (2) a simultaneous-contrast / flat-in-shadow correction, (3) generalization by landform, not blur.

226 Hachures and Other Related Techniques

Figure 152. General mountain hachuring, scale 1:900,000, enlarged to 1:450,000. Area: Central Switzerland.
From Stieler’s Handatlas, 1891.
With a few exceptions, old single color woodcuts and copper engravings were printed in
black and this was also applied to old landscape maps. This practice remained in most of the
single color copper engraved hachured maps of last century. The classical forms of the Swiss
Dufourkarte, the Deutschen Reichskarte (Map of the German Reich) at 1: 100,000, the
Osterreichischen Spezialkarte (Austrian Special Map) at 1:75,000, the Carta topografica
d’Italia (Topographic Map of Italy) at 1: 100,000, and the Carte de France (Map of France)
at 1:80,000 were all printed in black.
It is a violation of historical accuracy, and of graphic sensitivity, to produce facsimile
reproductions of old wood-cut and copper engraved maps in brown or in colors other than
black, a practice which, unfortunately, often occurs. The black hachure has stronger,
sharper, truer, and, above all, a more three-dimensionally expressive effect. The lighter the
printing color, the weaker is the light-dark contrast. Brown hachures do not fully exploit all
gradation possibilities; they frequently appear too weak, flat and fuzzy, andare often lost inthe
confusion of other map symbols.
In spite of this, one does not wish to lose the opportunity of employing brown, grey OF
similar colors for hachures in new mapsvsince they are necessary in order to achieve a clear,
visual separation of the different cartographic elements. Black text and symbols stand out
from non-black hachuring more effectively than from hachures printed in black. This advan-
tage often carries more weight than that of the better three-dimensional effect of black
A saturated neutral brown is suitable for hachures, since this distinguishes itself not only
from the black of text and symbols, but also from the blue of drainage lines and from the red
of roads and railway lines. The weaker the brown tone, the flatter and less vivid is the relief,
but the remaining elements contained in the map become more legible. The stronger the

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF' ### Ch.10 Hachures (p.213-234) - Slope hachure FIVE RULES (p.214-218): 1 dense zones of small parts of slope lin
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF'

### Ch.10 Hachures (p.213-234)

- Slope hachure FIVE RULES (p.214-218): 1 dense zones of small parts of slope lines, direction = steepest
  gradient (perpendicular to contours); 2 arranged in horizontal ROWS (sections) to keep density
  constant; 3 length = horizontal equivalent between (assumed) contours (l = k cot a); 4 tone "the
  steeper the darker" by stroke THICKNESS, (thickness + gap) constant; Lehmann black:white = a : 45-a
  (0 deg 0:9 ... 45 deg 9:0); Mueffling a:60-a; Austrian a:80-a capped at 48 deg (6:4, never black:
  lighter, suited alpine terrain); 5 same number of hachures per cm everywhere, chosen per map.
  Consecutive rows not connected, offset. Density per cm: 13-40 /cm; finer for smaller scale.
  Coarse patterns: harder, woodcut-like, clearer, more transparent; fine: smoother, flatter.
  -> PIXELS: 1 stroke + >=1 gap = period 2 is the max density; stroke weight can only be 1 or 2 px;
  so hachure tone in pixels is carried by LENGTH/GAP (dashes) and by stroke/space ratio with period
  3-5 px. Lehmann's "constant pitch, varying weight" is the pixel artist's constant-pitch dither.
- p.221 draftsman must pull hachures out of one row into the neighbour at sharp dips; "Congestion,
  knots of strokes, clogging ... indicate poor work."
- p.222-223 misrepresentations: dark steep areas abutting on ridges need white separation strips ->
  widened flat ridges; rigid geometry can't follow fine forms; unrealistic terracing; "The evil became
  standardized, the standard procedure became doctrine, the doctrine became the ideal."
  Figs 149 (slope) / 150 (shadow hachuring) Uetliberg: 150 = light side fine, shadow side bold; no
  white strips needed "since ... good detailed form is obtained through the change from light to
  shaded slopes". Fig.150 is a master-copy candidate (clean line art, survived the scan).
- D. General mountain hachuring small scale (Figs 151-153): freer; stroke rows drop; broad strokes.
- E. colours: black hachures truest; brown for separation from black text.
- H. Horizontal hachures (form lines), I. Tanaka planes, K. Eckert dots: skimmed.

### Ch.11 ROCK DRAWING (p.235-)

- A: no single form suits all rocks and scales; "Generally speaking, there are no ideal solutions to
  the problem of steep rocks." Find the best possible for each situation.
- B.1 (p.236-237, Fig.159 glacial alpine landscape in oblique pen): U valleys, cirques ("Kare") eaten
  back, sharp residual crests, horn peaks ("Karling"). Fig.159 is itself a model of oblique rock
  drawing: all slopes are fall-line strokes, dense on shadow sides; crests are where stroke fields
  meet; valley floor near-empty.
- B.2 GEOLOGICAL STRUCTURE (p.238-243), Figs 160-169 (Imhof's pen sketches, perfectly preserved):
  160 homogeneous soft (flysch): uniform slopes, roof ridges -> few long fall lines.
  161 GRANITE: large masses, sharp needle spires, even coarse surfaces -> long vertical flutings,
      strokes following the steep joints, spires as clustered vertical wedges. (Enchantments!)
  162 gneiss: pyramidal, sub-parallel wedge-shaped projecting rocks, sharp roof ridges.
  163 crystalline strata inclined: asymmetric roofs, splintered layered slopes, jagged dip ridges.
  164 steep/vertical structure perpendicular to the wall: narrow deep parallel runnels, chimneys;
      ribs needle-like at the top (Aiguilles).
  165 HORIZONTAL BEDS: broad table summits, truncated peaks, banded stepped slopes; resistant beds
      vertical walls, soft beds indented; walls have vertical joint strokes between horizontal
      ledge lines; talus fans below each band. (YAKIMA CRB!)
  166 inclined beds: pulpit peaks, ledges at the layer ends.  167/168 vertical slabs: serrated.
  169 folded: the strata lines themselves drawn as bands.
  "The external forms of rocky walls ... clearly betray their composition and their internal
  structure."
- B.3 gullies (p.243-246): every irregularity on a steep wet slope becomes a channel; gullies with
  steep back walls and narrow outlets; subsidiary ridges between them run from main ridge down to
  triangular frontal facets (residuals of the old surface): Fig.170 "group of normal forms".
  Resistant rock -> gullies less incised. Fig.171 beds parallel to the wall -> wide shallow gullies,
  slab faces; 172 beds perpendicular -> precipitous gullies, buttresses; 173 horizontal beds -> hard
  beds form cliff with narrow clefts, soft beds benches; undermining -> bastions; 174 oblique beds
  deflect gullies.
- B.4 (p.246): rock gullies elongated, scoured, seldom stepped; steepest in upper reaches; ridges
  notched; contour twist greatest half-way down; "the debris mantle forms the local base level".
  Plan view: branching like a POPLAR (acute, near-parallel), not an apple tree (broad river systems).
- B.5 debris mantle (p.247, Fig.175): gully -> debris cone like an hourglass; 30-40 deg; fines up top,
  big blocks run out to the valley floor or foot of the opposite slope; "The limits of the cliff-base
  are sharply defined in most cases as they are covered by scree cones." Small gully often at the foot
  of the cliff where scree begins.

cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF'

- C. FORM ANALYSIS (p.251-252) -- the analysis before drawing: 1 DEMARCATION lines (limits of rock
  against scree, vegetation, snow reaching up into gullies); 2 RAVINE/GULLY lines (negative skeletal
  lines: from tops of debris/snow cones up into basins); 3 CREST lines (upper edges of cirque-like
  basins; main ridges thick, secondary finer). "The ravine lines ... and the crest lines ... divide
  what was formerly a confused, impenetrably complicated image into its principal regions, imparting
  form to the whole." 4 only THEN the finer internal-structure lines (strata, bedding, slides,
  folds): "secondary in significance ... subordinated to the overall portrayal of the topography."
  Negative lines elongated, always linked; positive lines irregular, crooked, broken, interrupted.
  The two meshes seldom touch except at basin mouths "in a sort of scissors-like fashion".
  "This method may seem extremely tedious. It is the method for beginners ... When skilled, the
  cartographer will find short cuts".
- D.1 rock contours: combing-out (Fig.176) flattens steps -> "more destructive than local
  condensation". Fig.177 Kleiner Drusenturm: OBLIQUE VIEW in cartographic rock drawing style (!)
  -- the model for our landscape/iso rock.
- D.2 skeletal lines (p.257): ledges, gully lines, cracks, clefts, ridge crests, step edges, slab edges,
  bedding planes; reinforce the edge lines lying on shaded slopes; increase light/heavy contrast
  upward (aerial perspective); ridges NOT smooth: "jagged natural appearance retaining their rough,
  often interrupted and twisted character".
- D.3 rock shading (p.257-258): large forms boldly (main lit side light, main shaded side dark,
  transitions medium); then every rib, gully, crack, layer "without distracting attention from the
  broader formations". Local light adjustment everywhere for small forms, esp. on main lit and main
  shaded sides ("otherwise lifeless"). STEEP SLOPES MORE STRONGLY SHADED ON BOTH SIDES than gentler
  rock. Aerial perspective: contrasts sharper high up; "When this ... is ignored, the foot of a
  cliff can easily look like the upper edge of the same cliff." Grey tone alone on small rock areas =
  "smudges or spots".
- D.5 SHADED ROCK HACHURING (p.259-263) -- "It is rock drawing."
  a hachures laid over the skeletal framework so their combined effect = tonal values of a
    (prepared or imagined) shaded image. Contours alone inadequate; need photos at different times of
    day, stereo, geology. Graphically freer than slope hachures; like a perspective pen sketch (177).
    "Uncertain drawing, indefinite arrangement of strokes in shapeless, incoherent miniature stroke
    fragments, or even unnatural, stylized, uncontrolled lines give away the beginner".
  b FIG.178 (p.260-261), a good / b poor:
    1 horizontal hachures, shading by stroke width; 2 slope hachures, rough strokes THICKENED AT THE
    TOP (sharp delineation of the step); 3 very narrow rock band = horizontal hachures, width-shaded;
    4 poor horizontal (shading doesn't follow the bends); 5 poor slope: top not emphasized ->
    "feathery"; 6 narrow layer as slope pecks -> "a pattern of pecks"; 7 cross-hatching = "cloth
    weaving, but not of rock", too dark; 8 thick outline -> "cut-up and wooden";
    9 GOOD interweaving: slope hachures for steep walls, horizontal for the ledges on soft layers;
    10 poor: no shading, tiny pecks, "confused, feathery, stippled"; 11 poor: rigid structural lines,
    cross-hatched, too dark; 12 GOOD steep scale-like buttresses: gullies smoothly and sharply drawn,
    running upward and meeting at acute angles; "Narrow, open, illuminated areas or edges conform to
    the shaded areas. RIDGE CRESTS ARE NOT DRAWN IN, the scale-like type of structure being brought
    out by the variation of light and shadow"; 13 poor: stylized feathery strokes, no area modulation;
    14 poor: ribs too hard, outlined; horizontal hachures on narrow steep rises = "leaning ladders";
    15 GOOD slab wall channelled by narrow gullies: whole surface a light medium tone of horizontal
    form lines; each gully "a crisp illuminated side paralleled by a well defined shadow";
    16 poor: form lines start mid-surface for no reason; 17 poor: shadow strokes for gullies with no
    illuminated strips on the opposite sides.
  c attitude (p.261-262): strokes follow horizontal features or slope; steep steps in bedded
    limestone = rows of short downslope strokes; flatter layers = horizontal; narrow steep slabs and
    crystalline pinnacles NOT ladders of horizontals: "a few characteristic crest lines and slope
    hachures"; schistose flat surfaces horizontal; alternate in step-like limestone; change direction
    only when the form demands; cross-hachures avoid (if needed diagonal, one direction dominant).
    Dipping strata via slope hachures -> "the area hachure then becoming a structural drawing".
  d 3-D shading by stroke WIDTH, NW light adapted to forms; "With every small bend in the stroke, an
    extremely accurate, simultaneous change in stroke width"; bent strokes without width change and
    straight strokes with width change are both wrong. Lit sides: "an extremely light distribution of
    fine hachures". EVERY tiny shaded section (fissure, gully, tooth, notch) "matched by a
    corresponding, light, unshaded surface on its opposite side" (erase the hachures there).
  e density nearly constant; tone by thickness not interval; overall "lightness and transparency.
    Rocks should not lie heavily in the map like lumps of coal".
  f "THE FORM LIES IN THE TRANSITION." "A firm outline round every surface element ruins the image
    ... as if constructed from hard wood." Skeletal lines are aids; seldom in the final image. "The
    impression of a sharp edge is produced not by means of ridge lines, but rather through the sharp
    change between adjacent light and shadow areas." Outline of a rock step: start each hachure
    crisply and firmly. Continuous outlines only in narrow sharp deep gullies. SAW-TOOTH CREST:
    "allowing very tiny shadows and light areas to cut across the edge of the ridge." No stylized
    twisted strokes; bedding cracks + some roughness are enough.
  g aerial perspective: contrast rises with elevation.
  h fit the small into the large: small ridges, gullies, steps, cracks, strata "so slightly that they
    become secondary to the effects of light and shade on the main surfaces".
  i generalization needs genesis knowledge; open out very steep cliffs (almost lines in plan) at the
    expense of neighbouring flatter rock; emphasize layering, hard/soft contrasts.
- 6 "steeper darker" rock hachures abandoned (walls meet at ridges indistinguishably).
- 7 colour: dark (black, dark brown, strong brown-violet, dark grey); weak tints fail.
- 9b "For relief maps, there is no better, or more natural a portrayal of rocks than by the
  combination of 'shaded' skeletal outlines or rock hachures with corresponding shading tones"
  (Plate 11, Fig.27b).
- 10 karren: light, contours ungeneralized; ROCHES MOUTONNEES (glacier-polished, Enchantments basin!):
  "the lightest, smoothest horizontal hachures possible" (Fig.188); grassy/scree/snow slope with
  protruding rocks: lightest horizontal hachures + occasional small isolated groups of slope
  hachures, thickened in the usual way (Fig.189) -- avoid "restlessness".
- 11 SMALL SCALES (p.272-273, Figs 190-192 Ortstock 1:50k -> 1:150k generalized vs photo-reduced):
  the space for hachures disappears; a simplified SHADED SKELETAL outline takes over; lit slopes can
  no longer bear hachures; the smallest shadows in gullies, cracks, notches = heavier generalized
  strokes. Under a magnifier: "a wood-cut ... with bold contrast effects in which the structure is
  composed of completely light and completely dark". "Drastic generalization and sharp contrasts".
  Combined with hill shading tones: "The rock drawing ... provides a final intense shadow tone,
  reduced to the smallest area in the form of lines." Remove all unnecessary strokes, esp. on lit
  slopes. -> THIS IS THE PIXEL SCALE RULE (region ~1 m/px).
- 12 training (p.273): draw rocks in nature at suitable distances, varied light; trace skeletal and
  structural lines over photos; copy good rock maps at successively smaller scales; then: skeletal
  image (hard pencil) -> shaded pencil tones -> transform tones into hachures -> rub away unnecessary
  strokes and ridge lines.
- E tools: scraper indispensable (lit edges, narrow lit areas, roughening hachures); stone engraving's
  grain suited the rough rock hachure better than smooth copper.
- F examples: 181-186 Bietschhorn (crystalline) / Vrenelisgaertli (bedded limestone); 190-192
  generalization; 193-198 old/new, 195 Rohn: skeletal lines too sharp -> stiff, wooden.

G. Critical examination and application ol, — different methods of rock drawing 279
G. Critical examination and applicatior. .:{ the different
methods of rock drawing
Today, there is still much controversy over the various methods of representing rocks in
cartography. Poor art work and imprecise reproduction can bring a basically acceptable
method of portrayal into disrepute. The main difficulty, however, lies in the problem itself.
As was pointed out at the beginning, there is no entirely satisfactory means of portraying
steep cliffs and finely detailed rock areas in a cartographic plan view.
It would be hopeless to attempt, even with contours, to portray the tower of a Gothic
cathedral from directly above, with its buttresses, cornices, secondary towers and other
elements of the structure, and then to expect such a drawing to both provide an expressive
image of the tower with all its associated forms, and also to reveal their geometrical relation-
ships and architectural structure. The result would be a chaotic confusion of strokes and
lines. One cannot expect broken rock areas with steep cliffs to be any different, and hence
all attempts at plan view representation must be compromise. The only real question is
which of the various means of depiction best serve the map user.
The following remarks are presented in favor of rock contours over rock hachures.
Rock hachures and rock skeletal lines give only the plan view structure and not the
relationships of elevation and steepness of slope. Following this argument it is therefore
inconsistent and thus ‘illogical’ or ‘unscientific’ to use two processes different in nature, in
the same map, that is, to depict rock-free terrain by contours, but to dispense with these in
rock areas and produce modelling effects with hachures. The omission of contours today
would be even less appropriate, since photogrammetry makes this element available in rock
as well as in adjacent land.
Considerations such as these may be posed at the desk, and may be absolutely justified in
theory, but nonetheless the assumption is that, in fact, rocks can be depicted by con-
tours, but that this does not hold true for steep cliffed and complex rock areas. It has already
been indicated, more than once, that blurred, chaotic tangles of lines are produced under
these circumstances, and nothing of value can be carried out with such an image. The
inherent metrical values (heights and slopes) cannot be appreciated, cannot be deduced or
even constructed from an image such as this. Hence the so-called ‘logic’ becomes senseless,
the ‘scientific approach’ becomes a farce. The map is not a product of logic, but rather an
instrument of practical use. But very steep walls are an exception, someone will interpose. In
fact, these ‘exceptions’ are found throughout the limestone and granite mountains of the
world, and are also widespread in the tablelands of arid regions.
The matter becomes even more problematical, however, on closer inspecti

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF' - G. critical (p.279-282): no satisfying plan-view solution for steep cliffs (Gothic tower from above); contour
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF'

- G. critical (p.279-282): no satisfying plan-view solution for steep cliffs (Gothic tower from above);
  contours can't characterize wild rock; rock hachures give overall image, clarity, character; some
  prefer contours "only because they are unable to produce a good rock hachure presentation ... foxes
  for whom the grapes hang too high!" Per scale: 1:50k rock hachures without contours "excellent";
  1:100k simplified rock hachures; with hill shading, "a crisp, finely detailed skeletal rock drawing"
  and the hachure fill can be omitted (Plates 11/12).

### Ch.12 Small landforms (p.283-294)

- TAPERING HACHURE (p.285-): exactly downslope; "drawn sharply and heavily at the upper edges of steep
  slopes, continue downward in a fine line and end in a point"; horizontal rows; too wide spacing =
  coarse unsettled, too dense = dull, blotchy. Tall slopes: split into several rows (strip per row).
  Width by light from upper left. "Crocodile teeth" (wide wedges) wrong.
  -> PIXEL MARK: a 1-px column 2-6 px long, darkest/2-wide at the top, fading to the lighter step at
  the tip: the scarp/cutbank/moraine edge stroke.
- dots for scree (p.291): fine dots high on steep upper slopes, big dots and blocks disordered at the
  foot; dots in lines down the slope; "one of the failings ... distribute dots too densely and too
  evenly" -> blackened maps. Dots don't touch contours. Lit side fine & open, shaded side dense &
  heavy (p.284).
- glaciers: freely arranged (not patterned) blue slope hachures for ice walls; crevasses short crisp
  lines tapered at both ends; systems crossing at shallow angles.
- landslides/torrent gullies "like fresh, open wounds": free tapering hachures, crisp at top.

### Ch.13 Area colours (p.295-324)

- p.296-298: nature's colours vary with season, hour, weather, distance; a map picks "the most
  generally representative or predominant aspect". Large scale: high-sun summer air photo as model,
  subdued; medium/small: "colours seen from ground level at corresponding viewing distances" --
  1:100,000 viewed at reading distance ~ landscape from 30-40 km: colours dominated by light and
  shade and aerial perspective (grey-blue veil); foreground rich contrast; far: everything light blue
  / blue-grey. The smaller the scale, the more general the colours.
- p.298-299: "The finer and more detailed the relief picture, the more sensitive it is to disturbance
  by a multi-colored mosaic of the surface cover" -> subdued ground tones; light yellowish-green for
  treeless, stronger blue-green for forest, light ochre/brown-red for rock and debris, all with aerial
  perspective gradation. VALUES matter more than hues ("correct and incorrect 'values'").
  Walensee 1:10,000 gouache (Plate 10, 1938) -- "free artistic interpretation of visual impressions
  gained during long walks".
- Hypsometric types: 1 contrasting (chaos: "what not to do"); 2 higher-lighter (shadow perspective,
  suits mountains with hachures); 3 higher-darker; 4 spectral green-yellow-brown (grass green read as
  vegetation; egg-yolk yellow destroyed atlases); 6 grey/violet tops compete with grey shading.
  TYPES 9/10 (p.306-307): with oblique shading: LIGHT scale; near=higher -> contrasts intensify
  upward, so the ground tone lightens upward: bottom greenish/grey-blue, blue-green, green,
  yellowish-green, greenish-yellow, yellow, light yellow, white (top). Type 10 = the same for slopes
  in strong shadow: at the TOP left/right (lit/shade) contrast strongest; at the BOTTOM they converge
  (veil). Modelled on "a color photograph of a sunny, summer landscape taken from an aircraft at a
  high oblique angle over Lake Lucerne". Yellow ground + grey-blue haze mixing.
  TYPE 11 (large/medium relief maps, p.307-308): middle steps somewhat stronger green, top grades
  through yellow to light PINK/orange/yellowish-brown (not white); ALL rock and scree get the reddish
  light tone (glaciers white). "Without the addition of pink, such maps would seem too cold and the
  yellow high areas unattractively dull. The pink tint of the uppermost sunny slopes harmonizes with
  the red added to the highest and strongest shadow areas." Distant peaks on serene evenings in soft
  pink; "Solely insensitive people apply this red too strongly ... falsely glowing alpine peaks seen
  in cheap landscape postcards." Valley floor = half shadow + its tint. Keep sequences LIGHT.
  TYPE 13 (small scale, p.309): white, light yellow, reddish-yellow, fuller brownish-yellow,
  yellowish-brown, greenish-brown/olive, brownish-green/olive, blue-green, greenish-blue strong,
  grey greenish-blue strong. Lightness gradation dominates hue; red placed in the MIDDLE.
EOF

Which types of portrayal or elements should be combined in the case at hand?
How should the individual element be depicted so that it provides strength of expression
not only for itself but also in the combined portrayal, and taking other elements into
account as far as possible?
Some elements give primarily metrical information, others serve mainly to provide expres-
sion and form for an image. One element allows small localized forms to be recognized and
can be seen only in small areas. This group includes contours. Another element provides a
good overall picture of large areas, e.g. hypsometric layer tints.
One should always combine those elements which are as different or as contrasting as
possible, both in the information which they contain and in their design charcteristics: in other
words, those symbols which supplernent each other well in what they represent and are
One should also consider the ways in which the colors of the combined elements may
harmonize. Things which contrast in information content should also be contrasting in color.
Those things which are related in their meaning should not have clashing colors. The most
important element, as far as the information is concerned, should stand out.
There is an infinite number of possibilities of interplaying the elements. Some are found
primarily in older maps, others in more recent maps. Many combinations are only suitable
for larger-scale, others only for smaller-scale, maps. Possibilities also depend on the repro-
duction process, the number of available printing color, etc.
There follow discussions on a number of particularly important situations which often
arise or are particularly useful.
a) Combinations for large and medium scale maps
Owing to their scale, these groups are characterized by regions of the terrain which are often
simple and homogeneous in places and have great differences in elevation relative to their
plan dimensions. This permits the use of equal contour interval systems. Cases 1~9, which
follow, belong to this group.

1. Contours and slope- or shading-hachures. This symbol, introduced by early mapmakers.
is still employed in the production of fine combined images but its employment is now 70
longer recommended. Contours present the metric form of terrain surface. Hachures, espe-
cially hachures constructed on the principle ‘the steeper, the darker’, attempt, in a less
effective manner, to do the same thing and so there is little sense in combining them.
Furthermore, it is difficult to get a satisfactory agreement between the two in their represen-
tation of form in newer, more accurate maps. Contours in modern large- and medium-scale
maps of mountain regions often twist and turn very sharply and suddenly. The stiff, reg-
ulated structures of slope and shadow hachures, however, are not sufficiently adaptable to
these twists and bends. If both elements must be in agreement, the only solution is to adjust
the forms to the

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF' ### Ch.14 Interplay (p.325-362) - Same kind of patterns laid on each other destroy each other; DIFFERENT kinds r
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> NOTEBOOK.md <<'EOF'

### Ch.14 Interplay (p.325-362)

- Same kind of patterns laid on each other destroy each other; DIFFERENT kinds reinforce (lines over
  flat tints good). If two line patterns must overlap: one open, large-meshed, strong; the other fine,
  dense, light.
- "POSTAGE STAMP CARTOGRAPHY" (p.327): tiny samples compress too much; what reads in a stamp-sized
  patch is unbearable over a sheet. -> WARNING for our 8x crop studies: always also judge the full frame.
- p.328: "When everyone at a conference shouts at the same time, no one can be understood." Vorarlberg
  map: pale blurry shading + dense black rock hachures = "a pale, lightly-colored pudding in which
  several lumps of coal are embedded". Topographers carry 1:5000 detail into 1:50k: "One cannot pick
  out the scree slope for the stones." Smaller scale -> surface cover subdued in favour of general form.
  "Cartography is the art of moderation ... the law of scale is in control."
- Forest tints disturb shading: lighten/open the forest, or print the forest plate WITH the relief
  (forest shaded too). -> forests in our region view must carry the terrain shading (aerial student's
  per-tree slope tone confirms).
- B.4: shading and contours must agree down to the finest detail (no "broad flat generalized" shading).
- B.5 skeletal rock drawing + detailed hill shading: excellent, if skeletal lines crisp, no extra
  hachures, strong brown/grey/violet. Fig.220 (Everest rock skeleton for 1:100k) + Fig.221 (its hill
  shading). "Rock hachures which are too thick are normally unsuccessful on the illuminated surfaces of
  steep rock slopes; they darken them so much that the intended modelling effects never materialize."
- B.7 LANDSCAPE PAINTING IN PLAN VIEW (Walensee 1938, 1:10,000 gouache 2 x 4.8 m): not a colour air
  photo (vague, flat, grey, momentary) but "the visual experiences of a landscape painter". Theme:
  shimmering green silvery-grey depths vs warm red-brown heights, woven together by the atmosphere;
  calm blue-green lake as contrast. Shadow tones India red, brown, grey, blue or deep green BY GROUND
  COLOUR; shadows lightened by reflected light but CAST SHADOWS STRONGLY DEEPENED; sunlit slope bright
  ochre / yellowish-red, in shadow deep red-violet. "the colored mosaic of ground cover influences the
  color of the depth effects but does not destroy them." Result: beautiful, "But the end result is not
  a map" -- shows how far naturalism can go.
  -> WE ARE MAKING PICTURES: Walensee is our genre; the map rules are the discipline under it.
- B.8 (Plate 11, 1:100k): shading tones not mouse-grey nor red-violet but HAZY BLUE-GREY; toward the
  upper reaches more intensive and REDDER (carmine), toward lower areas lighter and bluer. Blue haze
  restrained from climbing lit slopes (valleys, flats, low hills). Yellow tint graded like "negative"
  hill shading (lightened/removed in strong shade), lighter in valley floors; rocky lit slopes
  yellowish-red. "Shading tones should dominate the surface tones, they should not be allowed to be
  submerged by them. This is very important."

B. Combination of various elements of terrain representation 341
avoided except for glaciers. At scales smaller than J :500,000 rock skeletal line drawing is
omitted since here it would not represent the form, no matter how meticulous the depiction.
In this type of representation it is important to maintain a perfect reflection of form and
the greatest possible harmony in the interplay of colors between the linear and areal relief
elements. Contours and rock skeletal lines are designed to contain more metrical informa-
tion for the map and to support and reinforce the three-dimensional impression of color and
shading. Rock hachures should be sufficiently faint and restrained on the illuminated slopes
that they do not destroy the impression of three-dimensional form. Area tones should be
transparent so as not to obscure the line image.
Dense cones of contours in a red-brown color and dark brown rock outlines change the
impression of area colors. This can be observed by comparing Plates 11 and 12. The area
tints in both are identical, the differences in appearance being solely attributable to the
strong effects of the added line patterns in Plate 12. These influences should be considered
when selecting printing colors. A medium, dull, bluish-brown or brown-violet is better
suited for rock depiction than black, since such impure tones move them back from the
black text and match them in with the area tones more neatly in a balanced picture. Dense,
black patterns of lines and strokes would spoil the soft areal tones.
The type of representation described here was developed many decades ago, primarily in
Switzerland. It is often called the ‘Swiss style,’ but is in no way limited to Swiss or alpine
terrain. It is suitable for all regions having a generally rough, ruggedly undulating terrain. It
is also useful for flat, gullied table lands and for moderate mountainous and hilly terrain,
moraine landscapes, etc. Since it combines the advantages of a metrical line scheme with the
direct impression of naturalistic colors and shading tones, it is superior to all other types of
representation. It is, in other words, the ideal landscape relief map.
One has come across this type of map in a variety of interpretations in technique and
stages of development. Earlier, strong brownish, reddish or yellow-reddish ground-surface
and illuminating tones dominated the usually pale shadow-tones — often too much so — such
that the latter sank or were immersed in the dominant elements and were inaccurate, the
old, traditional combined shading being always used. Valley bottoms and flat land were
therefore lighter than the adjacent illuminated slopes. Maps such as these were often ruined
by too strong yellow and pink tints; also the hypsometric blue was delimited too crudely or
abruptly, or even omitted altogether. A direct three-dimensional impression can only be
produced by consistent oblique shading, by the priority of hill shading over the surface
toning, by the most prec

== enchant
# lane-slide-shot: the landslide (and avalanche) coupling shot, results log

Branch lane-slide-shot (from master, with lane-reel 28eb53a merged in for vox-reel).
All builds/renders: `taskpolicy -b`, RAYON_NUM_THREADS=4, CARGO_BUILD_JOBS=2,
CARGO_TARGET_DIR=~/work/voxsim/target-slide-shot. Logs in runs/slide/.

## Why v1's slide never showed (diagnosis)

1. Wrong trigger: the slope's frame had no trigger_landslide option, the beat's
   text match fell through to "slump a small section" (~5 m3).
2. Invisible motion: geo's mass-flow solver takes voxels OUT of the world while
   they move (parcels) and lays them down when they stop. The painter only sees
   the world, so a slide painted as: slope quietly empties, deposit appears.
   Same for the avalanche (on top of snow-over-snow).
3. Material contrast: gravel scree sliding over gravel scree, or forest-floor
   soil over forest floor, is invisible at 0.2 m/px even when it runs 90 m.
4. Dry soil slides on these slopes stop after 2-4 m (landslide lambda 0.3);
   only the gravel ones ran far.

## What changed

- vox-play tools: `Cmd::Slide {x,y,r}` (geo `trigger_failure`: every connected
  loose column within r on ground >10 deg goes to its failure plane; debris flow
  if saturated, else landslide; physics decides the rest). Explorer key L.
  `Cmd::Soak {x,y,r}` (a cloudburst: loose cover in the disk to moisture 255 =
  geo's water table at the surface).
- vox-reel: `slide` / `soak` shot actions; `flowfx`: moving parcels drawn into
  the world for each painting (stacked per column = the solver's flow
  thickness; airborne at their position), then removed and change logs
  rewound; dust/powder puffs born from fast parcels (render-side). Voxels are
  handed to the paint mirror every frame while mass is moving.
- vox-reel `slides` subcommand: ranks trigger sites (volume, drop, green cover,
  big animals / people in the run-out), thumbnails, runs trials with stills and
  a physics outcome log.
- vox-core (integrator-owned, minimal, marked): `World::change_mark` /
  `rewind_changes` so the render overlay leaves no change-log entries.

  (site (1204,184): 339 m3, runs 91 m, 9 ibex carried up to 56 m, 6 killed)
  but on camera it is grey on grey: invisible even with the overlay.
- Sheets: renders/reel/slide/slides-hi_pm (deleted after review; rerun
  `vox-reel slides reels/slide.toml --

# Imhof's model: *Cartographic Relief Presentation*, in its own terms

Eduard Imhof, *Kartographische Geländedarstellung* (de Gruyter 1965); English edition ed. H. J.
Steward (de Gruyter 1982). Page numbers are the printed pages of the 1982 edition ("p."). The copy in
the library is a 1-bit scan: every line figure survived intact, every halftone plate (the shading
figures, the photographs) is thresholded to blotches, and the colour plates are not in the scan at
all. So everything below about tone and colour is taken from the text, and I checked the look of it
against swisstopo's current maps, which descend directly from Imhof's school. A full OCR text is in
`ocr/ALL.txt` and `library/text/imhof_cartographic_relief.txt`.

This file is the book's model: its principles, rules, procedures and vocabulary, as Imhof states
them. My translations into pixels are in LESSONS.md.

---

## 0. The stance

- **The map is not a photograph** (p.44-52). A photograph is "a jumble of light and dark patches,
  whose tonal values often depend on all kinds of things which have no relation to form and
  position"; cast shadows obscure relationships; far areas fuse by accident of tone. A drawing makes
  similar things look the same, emphasizes the important and suppresses the unimportant (p.52).
  "Views of nature ... contain no lines. The drawn line is a human invention, a useful abstraction"
  (p.45).
- **Imitate the natural effects "as far as is possible with the graphic elements available"** (p.79).
  For showing form in plan, "by far the best and often the only indicator of form is the effect of
  light shining obliquely" (p.79).
- **Direct and indirect elements** (p.83-85). Direct: what works like nature (light and shade,
  aerial perspective, natural colour). Indirect: conventions you must learn (contours, hachures,
  "the steeper the darker", layer tints). Only their interplay satisfies every requirement.
- **Good or bad, not right or wrong** (p.24). "The solution of a mathematical problem is either right
  or wrong, but the solution of a problem in cartography is, within certain limits, only good or bad."
  Keeping the true form (A-A in Fig.15) is usually better than keeping every detail in the wrong
  place (B-B).
- **Sketching means leaving things out** (p.45). "Leaving out, however, does not mean sketching
  inaccurately. Uncertain scribbling, formless or distorted hachuring, dot patterns, lines of scree
  dots, etc. betray inability. We should be able to see the wood in spite of the trees."
- **Beauty and clarity coincide** (p.72, 359). "A clear map is beautiful as a rule." "Uninterrupted
  noisy clamor impresses no one. Activity set against a background of subdued calm strength produces
  a deeply expressive melody."
- **Cartography is the art of moderation** (p.328): "When everyone at a conference shouts at the
  same time, no one can be understood."

## 1. Vocabulary

| term | meaning (page) |
|---|---|
| **slope shading** | tone by "the steeper, the darker"; flats white; no aspect (p.162) |
| **oblique hill shading** | tone by light from an assumed oblique, **diffuse** light; flats get a mid tone (p.166-171) |
| **combined shading** | the hachure-map inheritance: oblique light on the mountains, "steeper darker" elsewhere, flats white (p.194) |
| **"vertical illumination"** | the false theory behind slope shading; "bored like a woodworm into ... cartographic writing for over one hundred years" (p.162) |
| **expressionless** | a curved surface that receives one uniform tone, so its curvature is invisible (p.173) |
| **local light adjustment** | letting the light "wander" along slopes and around masses, up to 30° from the main direction (p.174-175) |
| **aerial perspective** | with distance, darks lighten, lights dull, contrast falls, everything goes grey-blue; in a map *high = near* (p.172) |
| **skeletal lines** | the ground plan of the terrain's edges: crests, gully lines, breaks of slope, terrace and slab edges (p.105) |
| **positive / negative skeletal lines** | crests (erratic, broken, interrupted) / gullies (elongated, always linked, branching upward) (p.105, 252) |
| **demarcation lines** | where rock ends against scree, vegetation or snow (p.251) |
| **slope hachure** | Lehmann's strokes down the fall line, in rows between assumed contours, thickness by slope (p.214) |
| **shadow hachure** | the same strokes, thickness by oblique light (p.224) |
| **rock hachure** | free strokes that follow the rock's structure and together make the tone of a shaded image (p.259) |
| **tapering hachure** | a wedge stroke for a small steep slope: heavy at the top edge, fine point at the foot (p.285) |
| **form-line effect** | parallel layers exposed by a surface show as lines, spreading as the surface becomes parallel to them (p.146) |
| **generalization** | making a new, legible form for the scale, not reducing the old one (p.126-134, 188) |
| **postage-stamp cartography** | tiny samples drawn so dense that the same density over a whole sheet is unbearable (p.327) |
| **Swiss style** | contours + rock drawing + oblique shading + aerial-perspective colour tones: "the ideal landscape relief map" (p.341) |

## 2. The problem of scale (Ch.5, p.75-86)

- The same kind of land at different scales (Figs 28-32, p.77): at 1:5,000 little is seen from
  above; at 1:25,000 rocky ridges collapse ("vertical cliff steps shrink together in plan"); at
  1:100,000 the rise and fall is most expressive; at 1:1,000,000 and smaller the land is detailed but
  flat. "Without supplementary imaginary modes of expression and ... drastic simplification and
  exaggeration of form, no image could be produced which has any value."
- Depth cues (p.78): thirteen of them, of which the one that makes structure strokes work is no.11,
  surface structures related to the form, "so that bending of the normal structure permits
  recognition of undulations".
- The model experiment (Fig.33, p.81-83): one cone in a ring-shaped groove, drawn eight ways. Slope
  shading gives cone and groove the same tone; contours can't say cone or crater; layer tints make a
  pipe or a tunnel; **oblique shading alone makes you see the form without a key**; shadow hachuring
  and combined shading contradict themselves where flats meet sunny slopes.

## 3. Lines: contours, skeletal lines and their generalization (Ch.7-8)

- **Legibility** (p.113): a line about 0.1 mm wide wants a gap about 0.4 mm; about 2 lines per mm is
  the limit.
- **Density is the form signal.** Mixed-interval systems are rejected because "all parts of the
  terrain, whether flat or steep, appear to be equally covered ... any suggestion of shape, produced
  by the grouping and spacing of these contours, is lost" (p.123).
- **Large scales want sparser than possible, small scales denser than theory** (p.116-119): the ideal
  interval grows non-linearly with scale; at large scale "the high density and overloading ... go
  against good perception".
- **The eight rules of generalization** (p.132): (1) generalize surfaces, not lines; never one line
  without its neighbours; (2) smooth out details not echoed in neighbouring contours; (3) neighbours
  never touch except at cliffs; (4) neither gently wavy nor jagged everywhere; (5) first transfer the
  crests and breaks of slope as guides; (6) a form crossing several contours is kept in all or
  dropped in all; (7) keep the prominent parts; broaden the larger negative form at the expense of
  smaller positive ones; (8) move lines only as much as legibility needs. "Inaccurate, incompetent
  drawing should never be excused on the grounds that it is presumed generalization."
- **Poor vs good** (Figs 76-85): poor generalization rounds everything into blobs (young sharp gullies
  and old flat surfaces become alike) or replaces every curve by a corner; good generalization keeps
  crests as crests, gullies sharply cut, limestone ribs angular.
- **Skeletal lines** (Ch.7): negative lines branch upward like a leafless crown; positive lines are
  erratic; on rock the gullies branch like a **poplar** (acute, nearly parallel), not like an apple
  tree (p.246). Drawn crest lines are usually a mistake: "The careful representation of a rocky area,
  with sharp contrast between light and shade, brings out the ridge much more clearly than would the
  crest line" (p.108).
- **The line of slope is natural; the horizontal line is not** (p.148): "Erosion and deposition always
  work in the direction of greatest slope. Along with the edge line, the line of slope is a
  characteristic producer of the naturalistic image." (The exception is rock that is itself layered:
  the form-line effect, p.146.)
- **Illuminated contours** (Figs 107-112, p.150-154): on a mid-grey ground, contours white on the lit
  side and black on the shaded side, the width following the light; works on favourable forms,
  stepped look elsewhere. **Shape lies in the transition** (p.143).

## 4. Tone: shading and shadows (Ch.9, the core)

### 4.1 The procedure (p.169-170, rules a-l)

1. Sketch all edge and skeletal lines first: the framework.
2. Choose the main light azimuth after studying the whole area. **Never parallel to the dominant
   ridges and valleys**; cross them at an acute diagonal. Generally from NW, W or SW. Mark where local
   deviations will be needed.
3. Cover all significant **horizontal surfaces with a medium grey** (valley floors, terraces,
   plateaus). "This medium tone is vital."
4. Shade the steep slopes facing away from the light; then lighter tones on flatter slopes and those
   only slightly turned away.
5. Work outward "around", "over" and "through" the ridges and hollows; slopes that the light grazes
   get intermediate tones by gradient. At the end only steep slopes directly facing the light are
   free of tone.
6. Use small local changes in the light for inflections the main light misses.
7. Start light; develop the contrast gradually.
8. **The transitions.** "The form depends upon these transitions. Sharp ridges require a sudden and
   crisp change from light to dark, while rounded landforms require softer shading transitions." An
   even slope is one homogeneous tone. "The dark bands or strips, which are drawn lovingly along the
   shaded sides of terrain ridges ... are meaningless and contrary to the form."
9. Large forms badly placed to the light: bring out their **secondary forms**. "The three-dimensional
   impression is not evoked by any individual grey tone, but rather by a spontaneous visualization of
   the entire scheme of shading."
10. A plane keeps one base tone, but may be pushed lighter or darker toward one side for contrast with
    its neighbours ("gives the forms more life").
11. Draw every small inflection precisely, but emphasize the larger relationships.
12. Check the drawing in a **mirror**.

### 4.2 Rules about light (p.171-185)

- **Flats carry a mid tone** (p.171). Without it lit slopes can't be told from flats; it "binds and
  subdues and permits small colored features ... to be brought out".
- **Diffuse light always** (p.168): softness and transparency of shadows.
- **Cast shadows, reflected light and highlights are left out of a map** (p.171-172): cast shadows
  have "no formal relationship with the local terrain"; reflected light leads to errors of
  interpretation; highlights distort. (All three are allowed in "works of art", and his own Walensee
  painting uses deepened cast shadows and reflected light: p.337.)
- **Aerial perspective by elevation** (p.172-173, 187): high = near, so sharpen light/shadow contrast
  toward the summits and soften it toward the lowlands; only with large elevation differences, with
  discretion; overdone, even slopes look concave. It also prevents relief inversion.
- **Local light adjustment** (p.173-177, Figs 130-131): a single parallel light leaves some ridges
  bold and others expressionless (Perron's Swiss model under W light: N-S ranges bold, E-W chains
  "dissolve into many small patches"). Let the light wander, **at most 30°** from the main direction,
  never wildly different on adjacent parallel slopes. Where a curving ridge turns its shadow side over
  (Churfirsten, Fig.131,5-6), work up the crest breaks and secondary forms. The drawn shading (Fig.130,6)
  "unifies the multitude of forms appearing in the three model photographs". "Better no relief
  drawing than a poor one."
- **Vertical light angle adapted to the forms** (p.177): flat undulating land, under 20°; steep shaded
  slopes need more than 45° or they black out; steep lit slopes want a flatter light so the steepest
  are the lightest.
- **Against formulae** (p.177-178): Wiechel's 1878 H = cos ε with a fixed NW 45° light (flat = mid
  tone) — which is exactly today's GIS hillshade — is "quite meaningless": "Three-dimensional effects
  do not result from the degree of local shading but rather from the interplay of tones."
- **Light from the upper left** is a right-hander's writing and drawing habit; true south light makes
  most viewers see the relief inverted; **west or south-west light**, "not unlike the commonly
  observed effect of sunlight in late afternoon or evening", is his recommendation (p.178-183).
- **Leonardo** (p.185): "between light and dark there exists a hybrid area ... a bright shadow or a dim
  light. This you must seek." The steep dark and steep lit slopes are easy; "the critical part ... is
  what lies between the two". "One and the same grey tone ... can appear in one place as a shadow and
  in another as light." Tones that collapse into "solid or non-existent" destroy every form.
- **Four difficult cases** (p.186): (a) slopes facing the light are lit all over: catch every gentle
  curve with small light changes; (b) steep lit / gentle shaded scarplands: keep the steep lit slopes
  light and the gentle shaded ones not too dark, sharpen small bends, darken neighbouring flats; (c) a
  small steep cone beside a broad eminence looks too low, because "the impression of height is ...
  also dependent on the sizes of the areas": overemphasize the small; (d) a small counter-slope in a
  big shadow reads as a sharp ridge if lit to its form: subdue it. "It is the overall impression of
  form which is important and not a strict adherence to some geometric law of shading."
- **Emphasis on large landforms** (p.187): (1) grade by elevation (aerial perspective); (2) "On the light
  side of a major watershed one should lighten all local shadows to a certain degree. On the shaded
  side ... strengthen them, the local illuminated areas being subdued further by application of a weak
  overall shadow which is not strong enough to destroy the detailed modulations" (Fig.132).
- **Generalization of shading** (p.188-190, Figs 133-134): photographic reduction fails ("cluttered,
  poor in quality and lacking in overall effect"). First smooth out gullies, niches, projections,
  terraces; then merge valleys and ridges; stress narrow but important valleys by tone; at small
  scales tone follows **elevation and relative relief**, not slope; exaggerate some heights; **keep
  the local character** (Alpine sharp ridge, Norwegian flat glaciated tops, volcanic cones). Poor:
  ravines that "devour" a tableland; stylized rounding; ridges "like water droplets ... with peaks
  standing up like masts".
- **Shading colour** (p.190-192): near shadows are grey; distant landscape shadows grey to pure blue:
  "select grey-blue, or blue-violet-grey shadow colors. Neutral grey is too lifeless while pure blue is
  too bright." Yellow, pink, red, red-violet can't model form.
- **Snow and glaciers** (p.192-193): don't shade snow more weakly (ridges become plateaus); "the
  shading tones should be used for the delineation of form alone and not to suggest the material".
- **Combined shading** (p.194-195): clear in high mountains, contradictory elsewhere; darkening steps on
  the lit side is "contrary to the natural effects of obliquely illuminated models".
- **Tools** (p.196-201): shading is built on a dense framework of guide lines. The pencil beats the
  airbrush: "Sharp and irregularly shaped terrain is always given too soft an impression by the
  air-brush ... Form is depicted more truly and more characteristically by the positive strokes of the
  pencil"; the airbrush is for flats, overall tones and aerial-perspective tones. Stroke direction:
  "On rounded land forms ... follow the direction of the contour with the pencil, while on sharp edged
  features ridge and slope directions are to be preferred." Watercolour is built from the lower levels
  up toward the ridges. "The art of 'scraping out' is just as important as the art of drawing in."
  Grey paper: start from the flat mid tone, darken the shadows, scrape out the lights.
- **Model photography and the computer** (p.205-212): models and single parallel light give
  expressionless areas and "a chaotic confusion of individual forms", and don't generalize. Of computer
  shading (his student Yoeli, Brassel, Hügli) he names three difficulties: (1) acute forms vanish and
  obtuse ones look acute under one parallel light, and the fix, "extremely delicate adaptation of the
  assumed light direction to the local topographical requirements", is "probably beyond the
  formulation of a programming logic"; (2) simultaneous contrast: "a flat area interrupting a dark
  slope ... will appear too bright ... could be interpreted as a rising slope"; (3) generalization: "The
  computer program, however, is 'blind'". His two suggestions: combine a second shading with the light
  turned 10-15°, and treat the computed shading as a working base to retouch by hand.

## 5. Hachures (Ch.10)

- **Lehmann's five rules** (p.214-218): strokes down the fall line; in horizontal rows between assumed
  contours; stroke length = horizontal distance between those contours; tone by **stroke thickness**
  with the pitch constant (black:white = a : 45°−a, black at 45°); the same number of strokes per cm
  everywhere. Consecutive rows are offset, not joined.
- **Shadow hachures** (p.224): the same construction, thickness by oblique light. No white strips are
  needed on ridges, "since excellent results and the depiction of good, detailed form is obtained
  through the change from light to shaded slopes" (Fig.150).
- **The failures** (p.222-223): dark steep slopes meet on ridges and need white separation strips, which
  widen and flatten ridges and valleys; rigid rows can't follow fine forms; separated bands create
  false terracing. "The evil became standardized, the standard procedure became doctrine, the
  doctrine became the ideal."
- Coarse patterns look hard and woodcut-like but clearer and more transparent; fine ones smoother
  and flatter (p.221).

## 6. Rock drawing (Ch.11)

### 6.1 Rock shows its structure (p.236-250)

"The external forms of rocky walls and mountain ridges ... clearly betray their composition and their
internal structure" (p.238). Imhof's pen studies (Figs 159-175) are the book's most transferable
pictures:

| figure | rock | what the drawing does |
|---|---|---|
| 160 | homogeneous soft (flysch) | uniform slopes, roof ridges: a few long fall lines |
| **161** | **granite** | large masses, sharp needle spires: long vertical flutings following the joints, spires as clustered wedges |
| 162 | gneiss | pyramids; sub-parallel wedge-shaped rocks projecting everywhere |
| 163 | inclined crystalline strata | asymmetric roofs, splintered layered slopes, jagged dip ridges |
| 164 | steep structure across the wall | narrow deep runnels and chimneys; ribs needle-like at the top |
| **165** | **horizontal beds** | table summits, stepped slopes: vertical joint strokes on the hard beds between horizontal ledge lines; soft beds recessed; talus below each band |
| 166 | inclined beds | pulpit peaks, ledges at the layer ends |
| 167-168 | vertical slabs | serrated ridges, slab walls |
| 169 | folded beds | the strata drawn as bands |
| 170 | gullies in homogeneous rock | "group of normal forms": gullies with steep back walls and narrow outlets, subsidiary ridges from the main ridge down to triangular frontal facets |
| 171-174 | gullies vs bedding | beds parallel to the wall: wide shallow gullies; across the wall: precipitous gullies and buttresses; horizontal: hard beds make cliffs with clefts, soft beds benches, undermining makes bastions; oblique: gullies deflected |
| 175 | debris | gully -> debris cone like an hourglass, 30-40°; fines high, big blocks run out to the valley floor; "the limits of the cliff-base are sharply defined in most cases as they are covered by scree cones" |

Rock gullies are elongated, scoured, seldom stepped, steepest in their upper reaches; the contours
twist most half-way down; "the debris mantle forms the local base level" (p.246).

### 6.2 Form analysis before drawing (p.251-252)

1. **Demarcation lines**: the limits of rock against scree, vegetation and snow.
2. **Gully lines**: from the tops of the debris and snow cones up into the basins.
3. **Crest lines**: the upper edges of the cirque-like basins; main ridges thick, secondary finer.
4. Only then the finer lines of internal structure (strata, bedding, slides, folds): "secondary in
   significance ... subordinated to the overall portrayal of the topography".

"The ravine lines ... and the crest lines ... divide what was formerly a confused, impenetrably
complicated image into its principal regions, imparting form to the whole." The two meshes seldom touch,
except at basin mouths "in a sort of scissors-like fashion".

### 6.3 Rock shading (p.257-258)

Large forms first and boldly: main lit side light, main shaded side dark, transitions medium; then every
rib, gully, crack and layer "without distracting attention from the broader formations". Local light
adjustments everywhere, especially on the main lit and main shaded sides ("otherwise lifeless").
**Steep slopes more strongly shaded on both sides** than gentler rock. Aerial perspective: contrasts
sharper high up; "When this graphic technique is ignored, the foot of a cliff can easily look like the
upper edge of the same cliff." Grey tone alone on small rock areas looks like "smudges or spots".

### 6.4 Shaded rock hachuring (p.259-263) — "It *is* rock drawing."

- Hachures laid over the skeletal framework so that together they make the tones of a (prepared or
  imagined) shaded image. Freer than slope hachures, like a perspective pen sketch (Fig.177).
- **Fig.178, good and poor forms** (p.260-261):
  - good: (1) horizontal hachures shaded by stroke width; (2) slope hachures, rough, **thickened at
    the top** to mark the step; (3) a narrow band as width-shaded horizontals; (9) slope hachures for
    steep walls interwoven with horizontals for the ledges on soft layers; (12) steep scale-like
    buttresses: gullies smoothly and sharply drawn, meeting upward at acute angles, narrow open lit
    strips matching the shaded areas, **"Ridge crests are not drawn in, the scale-like type of
    structure being brought out by the variation of light and shadow"**; (15) a slab wall channelled by
    gullies: the whole wall a light medium tone of horizontal form lines, **each gully "a crisp
    illuminated side paralleled by a well defined shadow"**.
  - poor: (4) shading that doesn't follow the bends; (5) slope strokes with no emphasized top,
    "feathery"; (6) "a pattern of pecks"; (7) cross-hatching, "cloth weaving, but not of rock"; (8) a
    thick outline, "cut-up and wooden"; (10) "confused, feathery, stippled"; (11) rigid structure lines
    and cross-hatching, too dark; (13) stylized feathery strokes, no area modulation; (14) ribs outlined
    hard, horizontals on narrow steep rises like "leaning ladders"; (16) form lines that start in the
    middle of a surface for no reason; (17) shadow strokes for gullies with no lit strips opposite.
- **Stroke attitude** (p.261-262): follow horizontal features or the slope; steep steps in bedded rock
  = rows of short downslope strokes; flatter layers = horizontals; narrow steep slabs and crystalline
  pinnacles are *not* ladders of horizontals but "a few characteristic crest lines and slope
  hachures"; change direction only when the form demands; avoid cross-hatching (if it must be, diagonal,
  one direction dominant). Dipping strata drawn with slope hachures make "a structural drawing".
- **Shading by width** (p.262): with every bend of a stroke its width changes with the light. The lit
  sides get "an extremely light distribution of fine hachures". **Every tiny shaded section — fissure,
  gully, tooth, notch — is matched by a light, unshaded surface on its opposite side.**
- **Density** (p.262): constant; tone by thickness, not interval. Overall "lightness and
  transparency. Rocks should not lie heavily in the map like lumps of coal."
- **"The form lies in the transition."** (p.262-263) "A firm outline round every surface element ruins
  the image ... as if they were constructed from hard wood." Skeletal lines are aids and seldom appear
  finished. "The impression of a sharp edge is produced not by means of ridge lines, but rather
  through the sharp change between adjacent light and shadow areas." The edge of a rock step comes from
  **starting each hachure crisply and firmly**. Continuous outlines only in narrow sharp deep gullies.
  A saw-tooth crest: "allowing very tiny shadows and light areas to cut across the edge of the ridge."
  No stylized twisted strokes.
- Aerial perspective (contrast up with elevation); fit the small into the large (small forms "so
  slightly that they become secondary to the effects of light and shade on the main surfaces");
  open out very steep cliffs at the expense of neighbouring flatter rock (p.263).
- Colour: dark (black, dark brown, strong brown-violet, dark grey) (p.266).
- **Glacier-polished rock** (roches moutonnées): "the lightest, smoothest horizontal hachures
  possible" (Fig.188); **grassy, scree or snow slopes with protruding rocks**: lightest horizontals plus
  small isolated groups of slope hachures, to avoid "restlessness" (Fig.189) (p.270-271).
- **Small scales** (p.272-273, Figs 190-192): the space for hachures disappears; a simplified **shaded
  skeletal outline** takes over; lit slopes can no longer bear hachures; the smallest shadows in
  gullies, cracks and notches become heavier generalized strokes. Under a glass it looks like "a
  wood-cut ... composed of completely light and completely dark". Combined with hill shading, "the rock
  drawing ... provides a final intense shadow tone, reduced to the smallest area in the form of lines".
  Photographic reduction of the large-scale drawing (Fig.192) is a tangle; the generalized redrawing
  (Fig.191) keeps the bands and gullies.
- **Training** (p.273): draw rock from nature at suitable distances and lights; trace skeletal lines
  over photographs; copy good rock maps at successively smaller scales; then: skeletal image in hard
  pencil -> shaded pencil tones -> turn the tones into hachures -> rub away unnecessary strokes and
  ridge lines. The scraper is indispensable.

## 7. Small forms (Ch.12)

- **Tapering hachure** (p.285): exactly downslope, "sharply and heavily at the upper edges of steep
  slopes, continue downward in a fine line and end in a point"; rows; tall slopes split into several
  rows. "Crocodile teeth" are wrong.
- **Scree dots** (p.291-292): fine dots on the steep upper slope, large dots and small blocks in
  disorder at the foot; lines of scree run down the slope; the common failing is dots too dense and too
  even. Oblique light: lit side fine and open, shaded side dense and heavy (p.284).
- Glaciers: freely arranged slope hachures for ice walls; crevasses as short crisp lines tapered at both
  ends; systems crossing at shallow angles (p.292).

## 8. Colour (Ch.4, 13, 14)

- **Perception is relative** (p.60): colour constancy under changing light; surrounds shift hue and value.
- **Brightness alone makes depth** (p.60-61, Fig.33): rings darkening to the centre read as a hole,
  lightening to the centre as a far tunnel; graded like "their appearance in nature when viewed in a
  landscape", colours give high and low: aerial perspective.
- **Six rules of colour composition** (p.72-73): (1) pure strong colours unbearable over large areas;
  strong colours for small extremes; (2) light bright colours side by side over large areas are
  unpleasant; (3) large backgrounds muted, greyish; grey the most versatile; (4) interweave the colours
  "carpet-fashion", theme colours scattered like islands; (5) one basic colour mood, as sunlight unifies
  a landscape; (6) steady, gradual de-emphasis; aerial perspective helps. Yellow light produces blue or
  violet shadows (p.70).
- **Natural colours** (p.296-299): a map shows "the most generally representative or predominant
  aspect". Medium and small scales look most natural in the colours seen from the ground at the
  corresponding distance (1:100,000 ≈ a landscape at 30-40 km): dominated by light and shade and the
  grey-blue veil. "The finer and more detailed the relief picture, the more sensitive it is to
  disturbance by a multi-colored mosaic of the surface cover." **Values matter more than hues.**
- **Layer tints for relief maps** (p.306-310):
  - types 9/10: a light scale in which the ground tone lightens upward so that light/shadow contrast
    can grow upward: grey-blue, blue-green, green, yellowish green, greenish yellow, yellow, light
    yellow, white; the shadow-side scale (type 10) converges with the lit scale toward the bottom
    ("veiling effects of aerial perspective"), modelled on a sunny summer high-oblique air photo over
    Lake Lucerne;
  - type 11 (large and medium relief maps): greener middle steps; the top through yellow to a light
    **pink, orange or yellowish brown**, and **all rock and scree get that reddish light tone**
    (glaciers white). "Without the addition of pink, such maps would seem too cold." "Solely insensitive
    people apply this red too strongly, thus introducing the effects of falsely glowing alpine peaks
    seen in cheap landscape postcards";
  - type 13 (small scales): white, light yellow, reddish yellow, brownish yellow, yellowish brown,
    olive, brownish green, blue-green, strong greenish blue, grey greenish blue. Lightness dominates hue.
- **The Swiss style's colour recipe** (p.340-341, Plates 11 and 14): shading tones "not dull, mouse-grey
  and dirty looking, nor ... stylized red-violet, or red-brown, but rather a **hazy blue-grey**. Toward
  the upper reaches ... more intensive and redder (more carmine), but toward the lower areas lighter
  and bluer." The bright haze blue on valley floors and low hills, restrained from climbing the lit
  slopes. The yellow light tone graded like "negative" hill shading (lightened or removed in strong
  shade) and lighter on valley floors; rocky lit slopes a yellowish red. **"Shading tones should
  dominate the surface tones, they should not be allowed to be submerged by them. This is very
  important."** Plates: strong dull brown (rock skeleton, scree contours), blue (water), red-brown
  (contours), **blue-grey (main relief shading), grey-violet (supplementary shading)**, light blue
  (haze on shaded slopes and lakes), **yellow (light toning)**, **pink, extremely weak (rock and high
  regions)**, black (text).
- **Walensee, 1:10,000, gouache, 1938** (p.336-337, Plate 10): the landscape painting in plan view —
  our genre. Not a colour air photo ("vague, flat, grey, blurred ... a momentary phenomenon") but "the
  visual experiences of a landscape painter". Theme: shimmering green, silvery grey depths against warm
  red-brown heights, woven together by the atmosphere, with a calm blue-green lake. Shadow tones
  "India red, brown, grey, blue or deep green" according to the ground under them; shadows lightened by
  reflected light but **cast shadows strongly deepened**; a slope in sun "bright ochre or yellowish red,
  but in shadow ... a deep red violet". "The colored mosaic of ground cover influences the color of the
  depth effects but does not destroy them." "But the end result is not a map."

## 9. Interplay (Ch.14)

- Patterns of the same kind laid over each other destroy each other; different kinds (lines over flat
  tints) reinforce. If two line patterns must overlap, one open, large-meshed and strong, the other
  fine, dense and light (p.326).
- **Postage-stamp cartography** (p.327): what reads in a tiny sample is unbearable over a sheet.
- Dense black rock hachures in a pale shaded map: "a pale, lightly-colored pudding in which several
  lumps of coal are embedded" (p.328). Topographers carry 1:5,000 relationships into 1:50,000: "One
  cannot pick out the scree slope for the stones." The smaller the scale, the more surface cover is
  subdued in favour of general form.
- Forests disturb relief shading: lighten or open them, or print the forest *with* the relief (p.332).
- Contours and shading must agree "down to the finest detail" (p.335).
- Skeletal rock drawing with detailed hill shading "can lead to excellent results" if the skeleton is
  crisp, without extra hachures, in a strong brown, grey or violet (Figs 220-221). Thick rock hachures on
  lit steep slopes "darken them so much that the intended modelling effects never materialize" (p.336).
- **On art** (p.359): "in cartographical affairs, as in all graphic work, the greatest clarity, the
  greatest power of expression, balance and simplicity are concurrent with beauty."

## 10. What the model is, in one paragraph

Terrain is shown by an imagined oblique, diffuse light from the west-north-west, adjusted locally so
that no important form is expressionless, over a flat mid tone that lets lit slopes rise and shaded
slopes fall; contrast grows with height (aerial perspective) and the large landforms are stated first,
with the small forms subordinated inside them; the form lives in the transitions — crisp on sharp
ridges, soft on rounded ones — never in outlines. Rock is drawn by its structure: first the demarcation,
gully and crest skeleton, then strokes that follow the rock's own structure (fall line on steep walls,
horizontals on ledges and bedded rock), whose weight follows the same light, each shadow matched by a
light strip, edges made by crisp stroke starts, the whole light and transparent. Every scale is a new
drawing, generalized by knowing how the land formed, and keeping its character. Colour is the light and
the atmosphere: yellow in the sun, hazy blue-grey in shade, warmer and stronger up high, bluer and paler
down low, and the shading always dominates the surface colours.
