Write: books/imhof/code/region.py
"""Region-scale (plan / map view) relief painter in Imhof's Swiss style, in pixels.

paint(z, cell, mats=None, **P) -> dict(rgb, idx, layers...)
  z     heightfield (m), rows north->south
  mats  optional uint8 material map: 0 ground, 1 rock, 2 water, 3 snow (default: rock by slope, water by
        flat basins)

Layers (MODEL.md 4, 6.4, 8):
  tone   imhof.imhof_tone: oblique diffuse WNW light over a flat mid tone, local light adjustment,
         vertical angle by slope, large-form emphasis, aerial perspective by elevation
  steps  tone -> 7 ramp steps; ordered dither only in the transitions
  colour ramps per material x haze bin: yellow light, hazy blue-grey shade; rock lit toward pink,
         low ground veiled toward light blue; shading dominates surface colour (p.341)
  rock   skeleton first: gully lines dark with a lit strip on their light side (Fig.178-15), crests
         never outlined; shaded rock faces get fall-line strokes one step darker (rock hachures, only
         where the pitch >= 3 px fits), lit faces stay open (p.262)
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as ndi
import imhof as I, skeleton as K, hachure as HA

# 7-step ramps, index 0 = deepest shade ... 6 = full light (sRGB)
RAMPS = {
    'ground': [(58, 62, 84), (80, 88, 104), (108, 118, 120), (142, 150, 132), (176, 180, 142), (206, 204, 154), (232, 226, 176)],
    'rock':   [(62, 60, 84), (86, 84, 104), (116, 112, 124), (150, 144, 146), (186, 176, 168), (216, 204, 190), (240, 230, 214)],
    'snow':   [(128, 140, 178), (150, 164, 198), (172, 186, 214), (196, 206, 226), (218, 226, 236), (236, 240, 244), (250, 250, 250)],
    'water':  [(24, 44, 58), (30, 54, 68), (36, 64, 78), (44, 74, 88), (54, 86, 98), (66, 98, 108), (80, 110, 118)],
}
HAZE = np.array([196, 210, 226])
MAT_NAMES = ['ground', 'rock', 'water', 'snow']


def palette(nhaze=3, haze_max=0.30):
    """pal[mat, hazebin, step] -> rgb. Haze bin 0 = highest (no veil), last = lowest (veiled, flatter)."""
    pal = np.zeros((4, nhaze, 7, 3))
    for m, name in enumerate(MAT_NAMES):
        r = np.array(RAMPS[name], float)
        for b in range(nhaze):
            a = haze_max * b / max(nhaze - 1, 1)
            mid = r[3]
            rr = mid + (r - mid) * (1 - 0.8 * a)          # contrast falls with depth (p.172)
            pal[m, b] = rr * (1 - a) + HAZE * a
    return np.clip(pal, 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)


def default_mats(z, cell, water_slope=1.0, water_area_m2=1500.0):
    mats = np.zeros(z.shape, np.uint8)
    mats[K.rock_mask(z, cell)] = 1
    s = I.slope_deg(ndi.gaussian_filter(z, 1.0), cell)
    flat = s < water_slope
    lab, n = ndi.label(flat)
    sizes = ndi.sum(flat, lab, range(1, n + 1)) * cell * cell
    big = np.isin(lab, 1 + np.flatnonzero(sizes >= water_area_m2))
    mats[big] = 2
    return mats


def paint(z, cell, mats=None, tone_kw=None, hachures=True, pitch=3.0, gully_area=300.0, seed=0,
          nhaze=3, dither_seam=0.35):
    z = z.astype(float)
    H, W = z.shape
    mats = default_mats(z, cell) if mats is None else mats
    T = I.imhof_tone(z, cell, **(tone_kw or {}))
    t = T['t']
    t = np.where(mats == 2, -0.15, t)                      # water: flat, a little below the land mid tone
    v = I.tone_to_steps(t, 7, mid=3.4)
    steps = I.quantize(np.clip(v, 0, 6), 7, seam=dither_seam)
    h = (z - z.min()) / (z.max() - z.min() + 1e-9)
    hb = np.clip(((1 - h) * nhaze).astype(int), 0, nhaze - 1)
    # ---- rock drawing: skeleton first, then strokes on the shaded faces
    rock = mats == 1
    g, acc = K.gullies(z, cell, area_m2=gully_area)
    g &= rock | ndi.binary_dilation(rock, iterations=2)
    # which side of a gully is lit? the neighbour with the higher tone
    lit_nb = np.zeros_like(g)
    for dr, dc in ((0, 1), (0, -1), (1, 0), (-1, 0)):
        sh = np.roll(np.roll(g, dr, 0), dc, 1)
        brighter = np.roll(np.roll(t, -dr, 0), -dc, 1) < t
        lit_nb |= sh & brighter & ~g
    marks = np.zeros((H, W), np.int8)                      # step offsets
    if hachures and pitch >= 3:
        wt = np.clip((-t + 0.05) / 0.6, 0, 1) * rock      # shaded rock only
        ink, _ = HA.hachure_ink(z, wt, sep=pitch, max_len=30, mask=rock, seed=seed, period=5)
        marks[ink >= 2] = -1
    marks[g] = -2
    steps = np.clip(steps + marks, 0, 6)
    steps[lit_nb & rock] = np.maximum(steps[lit_nb & rock], np.clip(steps[lit_nb & rock] + 1, 0, 6))
    pal = palette(nhaze)
    rgb = pal[np.clip(mats, 0, 3), hb, steps]
    return dict(rgb=rgb, steps=steps, mats=mats, tone=t, gully=g, hb=hb, acc=acc)
Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof/code; time python3 -c " import numpy as np, region as R, sheet as SH, imhof as I from PIL import Image z=np.load('../refs/enchant_aasgard_1m.
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof/code; time python3 -c "

Write: books/imhof/code/rockdraw.py
"""Shaded rock hachuring in pixels (Imhof Ch.11 D.5), plan view, for ~0.5-1.5 m/px.

The lesson of the copies (178-12, 178-15) and of Bietschhorn on the LK25: rock is drawn with strokes on
a light ground, not with soft tone. The tone field only decides the WEIGHT of the strokes:

  - strokes run down the fall line at constant pitch (evenly spaced streamlines);
  - on shaded faces a stroke is long and heavy (2 px on the side away from the light);
  - on lit faces it shrinks to a short tick at its upper end or vanishes ('an extremely light
    distribution of fine hachures', p.262);
  - gully lines (skeleton) are drawn first, dark, each with a scraped lit strip on its light side;
  - crests are never outlined: they appear where the stroke fields of two faces meet.

draw(z, cell, rock, tone, pitch=3.0) -> ink map: 0 none, 1 fine/tick, 2 stroke, 3 heavy, 4 gully,
5 lit strip (scraped)
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as ndi
import hachure as HA, skeleton as K


def draw(z, cell, rock, tone, pitch=3.0, light_rc=(-0.38, -0.92), gully_area=200.0, seed=0,
         lit_cut=0.35, heavy_at=-0.25, tick=2, max_len=24):
    H, W = z.shape
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    ink = np.zeros((H, W), np.uint8)
    lines = HA.streamlines(z, sep=pitch, test=0.62, max_len=max_len, smooth=0.7, mask=rock, rng=rng,
                           min_len=2.0)
    # heavy side: away from the light, in (row, col) pixel steps
    away = (int(np.sign(-light_rc[0])) if abs(light_rc[0]) > 0.35 else 0,
            int(np.sign(-light_rc[1])) if abs(light_rc[1]) > 0.35 else 0)
    for L in lines:
        if HA._bil(z, *L[0]) < HA._bil(z, *L[-1]):
            L = L[::-1]                                     # top first
        px = []
        for r, c in L:
            p = (int(round(r)), int(round(c)))
            if (not px or px[-1] != p) and 0 <= p[0] < H and 0 <= p[1] < W:
                px.append(p)
        if not px:
            continue
        tt = np.array([tone[p] for p in px])
        tm = float(np.median(tt))
        if tm > lit_cut:
            n = tick if rng.random() < 0.55 else 0          # lit face: a tick under the upper edge, or nothing
        else:
            n = len(px)
        for i, p in enumerate(px[:n]):
            t = tone[p]
            ink[p] = max(ink[p], 2 if t < lit_cut else 1)
            if t < heavy_at and i < 0.8 * n:                 # heavy: thicker toward the top (Fig.178-2)
                q = (p[0] + away[0], p[1] + away[1])
                if 0 <= q[0] < H and 0 <= q[1] < W and rock[q]:
                    ink[q] = 3
    g, _ = K.gullies(z, cell, area_m2=gully_area, scale_m=4.0, conc=0.02, min_len_px=6)
    g &= ndi.binary_dilation(rock, iterations=2)
    # the lit strip: the gully neighbour on the light side is scraped clean
    lr, lc = int(np.round(light_rc[0])), int(np.round(light_rc[1]))
    strip = np.roll(np.roll(g, -lr, 0), -lc, 1) & ~g
    ink[strip] = 5
    ink[g] = 4
    return ink, g

Write: books/imhof/code/place.py
"""Imhof's Swiss style applied to a voxsim place window: plan view and 30 deg iso.

paint_plan(z, cell, water=None, chm=None, snow=None, P) -> dict(rgb, steps, mats, tone, ink)
iso(rgb, z, cell, elev_deg=30, az_view=0) -> rgb of the painted map draped on the terrain, seen from the
    south at 30 deg (orthographic), painter's algorithm column by column.

Rules applied (MODEL.md):

- tone: oblique diffuse light from WNW, flats mid tone, local adjustment, big-form emphasis,
  aerial perspective by elevation (imhof.imhof_tone);
- rock: drawn, not shaded: light ground + fall-line strokes whose weight follows the tone, gully lines
  with lit strips, no crest outlines (rockdraw.draw); only where the stroke pitch (3 px) fits, i.e. the
  rock must be at least ~6 px across, else it falls back to tone (Imhof small-scale rule p.272);
- forest: carries the relief shading (p.332): forest ramp indexed by the same tone steps, crowns as
  single lighter pixels (chm local maxima) only on the lit side;
- water: flat, dark, its shore one step lighter (the lit lip);
- colour: yellow-warm lights, hazy blue-grey shade; haze bins by elevation (dithered).
"""
import numpy as np
from scipy import ndimage as ndi
import imhof as I, rockdraw as RD, region as R

RAMPS = dict(R.RAMPS)
RAMPS['forest'] = [(34, 44, 58), (44, 60, 64), (56, 76, 66), (72, 94, 70), (94, 112, 76), (120, 132, 84), (150, 152, 98)]
RAMPS['meadow'] = [(70, 76, 88), (96, 104, 104), (126, 134, 116), (158, 164, 128), (188, 190, 140), (214, 210, 154), (236, 230, 176)]
NAMES = ['ground', 'rock', 'water', 'snow', 'forest', 'meadow']
HAZE = np.array([196, 210, 226])


def palette(nhaze=3, haze_max=0.30, ramps=None):
    ramps = ramps or RAMPS
    pal = np.zeros((len(NAMES), nhaze, 7, 3))
    for m, name in enumerate(NAMES):
        r = np.array(ramps[name], float)
        for b in range(nhaze):
            a = haze_max * b / max(nhaze - 1, 1)
            rr = r[3] + (r - r[3]) * (1 - 0.8 * a)
            pal[m, b] = rr * (1 - a) + HAZE * a
    return np.clip(pal, 0, 255).astype(np.uint8)


def materials(z, cell, water=None, chm=None, snow=None, rock_slope=38.0, ground='ground'):
    import skeleton as K
    m = np.full(z.shape, NAMES.index(ground), np.uint8)
    if chm is not None:
        f = ndi.binary_opening(chm > 4, iterations=1)
        m[f] = 4
    rock = K.rock_mask(z, cell, rock_slope)
    m[rock & ~(m == 4)] = 1
    if snow is not None:
        m[snow] = 3
    if water is not None:
        m[water > 0] = 2
    return m


def paint_plan(z, cell, water=None, chm=None, snow=None, tone_kw=None, pitch=3.0, row_m=None, seed=0,
               ground='ground', nhaze=3, haze_max=0.3, rock_slope=38.0, ramps=None):
    z = z.astype(float)
    mats = materials(z, cell, water, chm, snow, rock_slope, ground)
    kw = dict(el_steep_shade=45, big_w=0.3, ap_low=0.85, landform_m=max(20.0, 20 * cell))
    kw.update(tone_kw or {})
    T = I.imhof_tone(z, cell, **kw)['t']
    wat = mats == 2
    T = np.where(wat, -0.2, T)
    st = I.quantize(np.clip(I.tone_to_steps(T, 7, mid=3.4), 0, 6), 7, seam=0.3)
    rock = mats == 1
    # rock areas too small to carry strokes fall back to tone
    lab, n = ndi.label(rock)
    if n:
        big = ndi.binary_opening(rock, iterations=2)
        keep = np.isin(lab, np.unique(lab[big]))
    else:
        keep = rock
    ink = np.zeros(z.shape, np.uint8)
    if keep.any():
        ink, _ = RD.draw(z, cell, keep, T, pitch=pitch, row_m=row_m, seed=seed)
        s2 = st.copy()
        s2[keep] = np.where(T[keep] > 0.0, 6, 5)
        s2[keep & (ink == 1)] -= 1
        s2[keep & (ink == 2)] = 3
        s2[keep & (ink == 3)] = 1
        s2[keep & (ink == 4)] = 0
        s2[keep & (ink == 5)] = 6
        st = np.where(keep, s2, st)
    # forest crowns: lit single pixels at canopy maxima facing the light
    if chm is not None:
        f = mats == 4
        c = ndi.gaussian_filter(chm.astype(float), 0.8)
        peak = (c == ndi.maximum_filter(c, 5)) & f & (c > 6)
        st[peak] = np.minimum(st[peak] + 2, 6)
        sh = np.roll(np.roll(peak, 1, 0), 1, 1) & f        # each crown throws a one-pixel shadow SE
        st[sh] = np.maximum(st[sh] - 1, 0)
    # water: lit lip on the shore facing the light
    shore = wat & ~ndi.binary_erosion(wat)
    st[shore] = 4
    h = (z - z.min()) / (z.max() - z.min() + 1e-9)
    hb = I.quantize(np.clip((1 - h) * (nhaze - 1), 0, nhaze - 1), nhaze, seam=0.9)
    pal = palette(nhaze, haze_max, ramps)
    rgb = pal[mats, hb, np.clip(st, 0, 6)]
    return dict(rgb=rgb, steps=st, mats=mats, tone=T, ink=ink)


def iso(rgb, z, cell, elev_deg=30.0, sky=(206, 214, 224), dark=0.0):
    """Orthographic view from the south (bottom of the map) at elev_deg. Each map pixel is a column
    whose screen y = row*sin(e) - height/cell*cos(e); painted far (top rows) to near."""
    H, W = z.shape
    e = np.radians(elev_deg)
    hz = (z - z.min()) / cell
    sy = np.arange(H)[:, None] * np.sin(e) - hz * np.cos(e)
    y0 = int(np.floor(sy.min())); y1 = int(np.ceil(sy.max())) + 2
    out = np.zeros((y1 - y0, W, 3), np.uint8); out[:] = sky
    top = np.full(W, 10 ** 9)
    ys = np.round(sy - y0).astype(int)
    # near rows occlude far rows: paint from far (row 0) to near, filling down to the previous surface
    last = np.full(W, -1)
    for r in range(H):
        y = ys[r]
        for_cols = np.arange(W)
        prev = np.where(last < 0, y, last)
        # fill from y down to the previous row's y (covers steep faces seen from the front)
        lo = np.minimum(y, prev); hi = np.maximum(y, prev)
        for c in range(W):
            out[lo[c]:hi[c] + 1, c] = rgb[r, c]
        last = y
    return out

"""Apply the method to the four place windows; plan + iso sheets beside the orthos."""
import numpy as np, sys, place as PL, sheet as SH
from PIL import Image
from scipy import ndimage as ndi
ORTHO = '~/work/voxsim-data/places/%s/ortho/%s_1024m.png'
SITES = {
    'enchant_aasgard': ('enchant', 'aasgard_pass', 'ground'),
    'yakima_reds': ('yakima', 'reds', 'meadow'),
    'sky_mtindex': ('skykomish', 'mount_index', 'meadow'),
    'sky_index': ('skykomish', 'index', 'meadow'),
}
names = sys.argv[1:] or list(SITES)
for name in names:
    slug, spot, ground = SITES[name]
    z = np.load(f'../refs/{name}_1m.npy').astype(float)
    chm = np.load(f'../refs/{name}_chm.npy'); wat = np.load(f'../refs/{name}_water.npy')
    ortho = Image.open(ORTHO % (slug, spot)).convert('RGB')
    out = {}
    for cell in (1, 2):
        zs = ndi.zoom(z, 1 / cell, order=1) if cell > 1 else z
        cs = chm[::cell, ::cell]; ws = wat[::cell, ::cell]
        snow = None
        if slug == 'enchant':
            o = ndi.gaussian_filter(np.asarray(ortho.resize(zs.shape[::-1], Image.BOX)).astype(float).mean(-1), 8 / cell)
            from imhof import slope_deg
            sl = slope_deg(ndi.gaussian_filter(zs, 3 / cell), cell)
            snow = ndi.binary_opening((o > 175) & (sl < 20) & (ws == 0), iterations=2)
            lab, n = ndi.label(snow); sz = ndi.sum(snow, lab, range(1, n + 1))
            snow = np.isin(lab, 1 + np.flatnonzero(sz > 2000 / cell ** 2))
        r = PL.paint_plan(zs, float(cell), water=ws, chm=cs if (cs > 4).mean() > 0.001 else None, snow=snow,
                          ground=ground, row_m=8.0 if cell == 1 else None, seed=1)
        out[cell] = r
    # iso of the 2 m map (512 px wide), from the south
    zs = ndi.zoom(z, 0.5, order=1)
    isoimg = PL.iso(out[2]['rgb'], zs, 2.0, 30)
    np.save(f'../refs/{name}_plan1.npy', out[1]['rgb']); np.save(f'../refs/{name}_plan2.npy', out[2]['rgb'])
    np.save(f'../refs/{name}_iso2.npy', isoimg)
    c = (slice(256, 512), slice(256, 640))
    rows = [[(SH.fit(ortho.resize((512, 512), Image.BOX), 1), 'NAIP ortho (2 m/px)'), (SH.fit(out[2]['rgb'], 1), 'Imhof method, plan, 2 m/px')],
            [(SH.fit(np.asarray(ortho.resize((1024, 1024), Image.BOX))[c], 2), 'ortho crop 1 m/px, 2x'), (SH.fit(out[1]['rgb'][c], 2), 'method, 1 m/px, 2x')],
            [(SH.fit(isoimg, 1), '30 deg iso from the south, 2 m/px map draped on the lidar')]]
    SH.sheet(rows, f'{name}: Imhof method on the real 1 m lidar', out=f'../sheets/place_{name}.png')
    print(name, 'done')
EOF

# What Imhof teaches, in pixels

*Notes toward a studio post. The book's model, in its own terms and with page references, is in
[MODEL.md](MODEL.md); my working thread is [NOTEBOOK.md](NOTEBOOK.md). Every sheet named here is in
`sheets/`; every script in `code/` or `copies/`.*

## 0. What I had to work with

- The library's copy of *Cartographic Relief Presentation* (1982 English edition) is a 1-bit scan.
  The line figures survived perfectly: Imhof's pen studies of rock types (Figs 159-175), the good and
  poor rock hachures (Fig.178), hachure systems, contours. **Every halftone and every colour plate is
  gone**, thresholded into blotches: the shading figures (118-120, 130-136) and the colour plates can't
  be seen at all. So for tone and colour I worked from Imhof's text, which is unusually exact, and
  checked the look against swisstopo's maps (the LK25 and LK50 rock drawing, the 1:500k relief, the
  Dufour map), which descend directly from his school. `code/swissfetch.py` fetches them and
  `code/dem.py` fetches the 2 m swissALTI3D DEM under them.
- I OCR'd all 405 pages (`ocr/ALL.txt`, and a copy for the library in `library/text/`) and read the
  whole book, looking at every figure page as I went.

## 1. The model in one paragraph

(From MODEL.md 10.) Terrain is shown by an imagined oblique, **diffuse** light from the west-north-west,
**adjusted locally** so that no important form is expressionless, over a **flat mid tone** that lets lit
slopes rise and shaded slopes fall; contrast grows with height (aerial perspective); **the large forms
are stated first** and the small ones subordinated inside them; **the form lives in the transitions**,
never in outlines. **Rock is drawn by its structure**: first the demarcation, gully and crest skeleton,
then strokes that follow the rock (fall line on steep walls, horizontals on ledges), whose weight
follows the same light, every shadow matched by a light strip, edges made by crisp stroke starts, the
whole light and transparent. Every scale is a new drawing. Colour is light and atmosphere: yellow in the
sun, hazy blue-grey in shade, warmer high, bluer low, and the shading dominates the surface colours.

## 2. Translations, one principle at a time

### 2.1 Fig.33: oblique shading is the only direct form (sheet `e1_fig33_cone.png`)

Imhof's eight renderings of one cone in a ring groove (p.81-83), redone at 96×96 px. The halftone plate
is lost, but the text says what each panel shows, and in pixels every one of his verdicts holds: slope
shading gives the cone and the groove one tone; contours can't say cone or crater; "higher darker" is a
pipe and "higher lighter" a tunnel; shadow hachures and combined shading make the lit wall of the groove
*darker* than the flat beside it (his "shadow reversal"). Only oblique shading over a mid-grey flat shows
the form without a key. The Lambert panel (Wiechel's 1878 formula, which is today's GIS hillshade) is
the oblique panel with no diffuse light, and its shadow side goes nearly black: Imhof's objection to
fixed-formula shading, visible at 3x.

*Carries directly.* The pixel rule: **flat ground is the middle step of the ramp, not the top one.** Lit
slopes rise above it, shaded slopes fall below it. Every studio ramp that puts flat ground at the top
step throws away half the light.

### 2.2 Tone: `imhof.imhof_tone`

Each of his lighting rules became one parameter:

| Imhof (page) | code |
|---|---|
| flats get a medium grey (p.171) | tone 0 = flat; lit and shaded are ± departures |
| diffuse light, "softness and transparency of shadows" (p.168) | `diffuse` share of sky light |
| light from W/WNW, like late afternoon (p.183) | `az=292.5` |
| never parallel to the main ridges | choose `az` per scene |
| local light adjustment ≤ 30° (p.174-175) | `local_light_az`: the landform's facing (read at `landform_m`) pushes the light away from grazing, same on neighbouring parallel slopes |
| vertical angle: < 20° on gentle ground, > 45° on steep shaded slopes, flatter on steep lit ones (p.177) | `el_flat`, `el_steep_shade`, `el_steep_lit` |
| lighten local shadows on the light side of a major watershed, darken the shadow side with a weak overall shadow (p.187) | `big_w`, `big_m` |
| sharpen contrast toward the summits (p.172) | `ap_low` |
| Leonardo: the half and quarter shadows carry the form (p.185) | `quantize`: flat inside a step, ordered dither only in a narrow seam at step boundaries |

On the Aasgard lidar (sheet `region_aasgard_v3.png`) the Imhof tone against plain Lambert does what the
book says: the NE face of Dragontail stays one dark mass but its gullies still read inside it; the shade
never goes black; the basin floor sits at the mid tone. **What did not carry as tone:** the same tone
at 1 m/px, quantised to 7 steps, becomes camouflage — blotchy islands of step 4 and 5 all over the gentle
ground. The fix was not in the tone at all; see 2.4.

### 2.3 Master copies of the rock drawing (sheets `copy_178_12.png`, `copy_178_15.png`, `copy_161.png`, `copy_165_r4.png`)

**Fig.178-12, steep scale-like buttresses** ("ridge crests are not drawn in"). My first copy placed
feathered barbs on hand-drawn spines: a row of combs — rigid, too regular, crests reading as outlines of
strokes. The copy that works came from doing what Imhof tells students to do (p.273): I placed only the
gully lines by hand, let them imply a surface, and drew strokes down that surface's fall line at a
constant pitch, weighted by the surface's light. The strokes then end against each other where they
converge into a gully — the feathering in the plate — and no crest is ever drawn. Measured on the plate:
stroke pitch 13 source px = **2.2-2.9 px at the size I copy**, median stroke 5 px, ink share 0.29; mine
0.36. What it doesn't have yet: his strokes are rough and slightly irregular; mine are clean.

**Fig.178-15, a slab wall channelled by gullies** ("a light medium tone of horizontal form lines; each
gully a crisp illuminated side paralleled by a well defined shadow"). The horizontal form lines are the
contours of the implied surface, broken into dashes at his pitch (~4 px); the gully is a two-pixel shadow
line with the pixel on its light side scraped clean. The chevrons where the form lines kink down into
each gully came for free from the geometry. This is the most transferable pixel mark in the book:
**a gully = dark line + one lit pixel beside it, never a dark line alone** (178-17 is the failure).

**Fig.161, granite** (masses and needles). A pen drawing doesn't copy into pixels stroke for stroke:
reduced photographically (his own Fig.192 failure) it is a tangle; transferred stroke by stroke it is
graffiti. It carries when translated into **values**: I read the masses off the pen's density (lit
fronts open, right flanks in shade), kept only his long flutes, a step darker on lit faces and a crevice
step in shade, each crevice with a lit pixel on its light side, contrast falling toward the foot of the
wall (his aerial perspective, p.263), a garlanded foot where snow tongues reach up the gullies (p.247)
and lit needle edges against the sky. At 1x it reads as a granite range; at 4x the flutes are still a
little mechanical.

**Fig.165, horizontally bedded rock** (the Yakima case). Four rounds. (1) A band chart with barcode
joints. (2) His strokes transferred one-to-one: pen graffiti. (3) A tidier band chart. Then I looked at
the plate at 11 screen px per pixel and saw what I'd missed: the lower wall is a row of **rounded pillars
with tombstone tops** bulging up into the bench, and the "trees" are gullies whose dark trunks branch
upward into the bench debris. (4) Built from those parts, it finally reads as his drawing. It's still the
weakest copy: the bench between the gully trees is too uniform, and the far plateau is too pale.

**What the copies taught, which I could not have got from the text:**

1. Rock in the Swiss style is *strokes on a light ground*. The rock is lighter than you think, and its
   darkness comes from the density of the strokes on the shaded faces.
2. Pitch: 3 px is the finest that reads; 4 px for form lines. At 2 px the strokes merge into tone.
3. The skeleton (gully lines) is the only thing placed by hand; everything else follows from it.
4. A pen drawing is translated through its values; the line itself doesn't survive reduction.

### 2.4 Rock at region scale on real lidar (sheet `rock_1m_aasgard.png`)

`code/rockdraw.py`: evenly spaced fall-line streamlines (Jobard-Lefer) on the rock mask, pitch 3 px; on
shaded faces long strokes, a second pixel on the side away from the light where the face is darkest; on
lit faces a two-pixel tick under the upper edge or nothing ("an extremely light distribution of fine
hachures", p.262); gully lines from flow accumulation × concavity drawn first, with a scraped lit strip;
no crest lines. Option `row_m` breaks the strokes into Lehmann's rows (p.214), as on the LK25.

Beside the swisstopo LK25 Bietschhorn at the same 1 m/px, variant A (strokes on a light ground) is the
one that looks like the Swiss drawing: the NE face of Dragontail becomes a field of feathered gullies,
dark by stroke density, and the lit slabs above stay open. Variant B (tone underneath plus strokes)
turns back into shading with texture on it. Tone only at 1 m/px is the camouflage mentioned above.

## 3. Tests where it matters

(filled in below as the place runs complete)

## 4. What did not carry, and why

- **Hachures as the only tone for everything (Lehmann/Dufour).** A pixel stroke has one weight, maybe
  two; the book's tone-by-stroke-width needs many. Fall-line hachures work for rock in pixels, not for
  meadow and forest, where tone does the job better.
- **Contours.** At 1-2 px/m a contour interval that reads (≥ 4 px apart) is 8-10 m on steep ground and
  vanishes on gentle ground; as a picture element they only work as form lines on rock (178-15).
- **Omitting cast shadows, reflected light and highlights** is a map rule for legibility (p.171-172).
  Our pictures keep them where a painting would (his own Walensee painting deepens cast shadows).
- **The halftone plates.** Not seen; everything about the look of his shading comes from his text and
  from swisstopo.

# Imhof method: code

All Python 3 + numpy/scipy/scikit-image/PIL; one core; a 1024x1024 window takes ~1-3 min (the
streamline placement is pure Python).

| file | what |
|---|---|
| `imhof.py` | tone: `imhof_tone(z, cell, **P)` (oblique diffuse light over a flat mid tone, local light adjustment <= 30 deg, vertical angle by slope, large-form emphasis, aerial perspective by elevation); baselines `lambert`, `slope_shading`; `quantize` (flat steps, dither only in the transitions); `tone_to_steps` (flat = middle step) |
| `hachure.py` | `streamlines` (evenly spaced fall-line strokes, Jobard-Lefer); `hachure_ink` (Lehmann-style dashed hachures weighted by a tone field) |
| `skeleton.py` | Imhof's form analysis: `flow_acc`, `gullies` (area x concavity, thinned, linked), `crests`, `rock_mask` |
| `rockdraw.py` | shaded rock hachuring: `draw(z, cell, rock, tone, pitch=4, row_m=None)` -> ink classes (tick / stroke / dark stroke / gully / scraped lit strip) |
| `place.py` | the whole Swiss style on a voxsim window: `paint_plan(z, cell, water, chm, snow, ...)`; `iso(rgb, z, cell, 30)` drapes the painted map on the terrain seen from the south |
| `region.py` | the earlier tone-only region painter (ramps, haze palette) |
| `dem.py` | `wa(slug, E0, N0, E1, N1, layer)` reads the voxsim place packs (1 m elev, chm, water); `swiss(...)` swissALTI3D 2 m |
| `swissfetch.py` | swisstopo WMTS mosaics (LK25/50/100/500, Dufour, relief) for reference |
| `run_places.py` | the tests on the four place windows -> `sheets/place_*.png` |
| `e1_cone.py`, `t_rock1m.py` | exercises -> sheets |
| `sheet.py`, `grid.py`, `pen.py`, `vectorize.py`, `cmp_copy.py`, `measure_ink.py` | tools for the master copies |

Parameters that matter (defaults in `imhof.DEFAULT`, `place.paint_plan`):

- `az` 292.5 (WNW). Turn it so it crosses the main ridge direction at an acute angle.
- `el_flat` 25, `el_steep_shade` 45-55, `el_steep_lit` 30.
- `local_dev` 30 (0 turns local adjustment off), `landform_m` 20-60 (scale of 'the landform').
- `big_w` 0.3, `big_m` 250: large-form emphasis. `ap_low` 0.8-0.85: contrast at the lowest point.
- `pitch` 4 px for rock strokes at 1 m/px (3 is the finest that reads; 2 turns into tone).
- `row_m` 8-10: Lehmann rows (a 1-px break each time a stroke crosses a row contour).
- `rock_slope` 38 deg: what counts as rock when no geology is given.
- `slab_interval` 2 m: glacier-polished slabs get the lightest broken form lines (Fig.188).

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF' ## 5. The rules, for students and lanes Each rule is Imhof's, with its page; the pixel form is mine. 1. **Flat 
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; cat >> LESSONS.md <<'EOF'

## 5. The rules, for students and lanes

Each rule is Imhof's, with its page; the pixel form is mine.

1. **Flat ground is the middle step** (p.171). Lit slopes step up from it, shaded slopes step down.
   Never put flat ground at the top of the ramp.
2. **Light from the WNW, diffuse, and bent locally** (p.168, 174-177, 183): one main azimuth crossing the
   main ridges at an acute angle; turn it up to 30° per landform so no important slope is lit
   side-on; steep shaded slopes get a higher light (they must not go black), steep lit slopes a flatter
   one (the steepest lit face is the lightest).
3. **Large forms first** (p.187): the light side of a major ridge gets its small shadows lifted, the
   shadow side a weak overall shadow; then the small forms inside.
4. **Contrast grows with height** (p.172, 263): full range on the summits, compressed in the valleys.
   In an oblique view real distance does this; in plan or iso use elevation.
5. **The form is in the transition** (p.170, 262): sharp ridge = the tone jumps across one pixel;
   rounded form = a dithered seam. Planes are flat steps. No outlines on ridges, ever.
6. **Rock is drawn, not shaded** (p.259-263): light ground; strokes down the fall line at a constant
   pitch (4 px at 1 m/px, 3 at the finest); dense and long on shaded faces, a two-pixel tick or nothing
   on lit ones; dark strokes only on the darkest faces, still 1 px wide.
7. **Skeleton first** (p.251-252, 273): gully lines placed before any stroke; each gully is a dark line
   with a lit pixel on its light side (178-15), never a dark line alone (178-17). Crests appear where
   two stroke fields meet.
8. **Rock by its structure** (Figs 160-175): granite = long flutes following the joints and needle
   silhouettes; horizontally bedded rock (CRB) = cliff bands of pillars/columns between ledge lines,
   debris benches and fans below each band; polished slabs = the lightest broken horizontal form lines
   (Fig.188).
9. **Small rock falls back to tone** (p.272): if a rock patch can't hold three strokes across, it's a
   shadow tone, not strokes.
10. **Shadows are hazy blue-grey, lights warm; shading beats surface colour** (p.190, 340-341): ground
    cover tints but must not break the relief; forest carries the same shading steps as the ground
    under it (p.332).
11. **Every scale is a new drawing** (p.188, 272): never box-reduce a detailed drawing; redraw with fewer,
    stronger marks that keep the character (sharp alpine ridges stay sharp).
12. **Economy is uneven** (critic, and p.262): dense where the form turns away from the light, empty where
    it's lit. Even spacing and even widths everywhere read as a machine.
EOF
echo ok

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof/code; cat >> rockdraw.py <<'EOF' def draw_bedded(z, cell, rock, tone, bed_m=6.0, pitch=4.0, light_rc=(-0.38, -0.92), seed=0, **kw): """
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof/code; cat >> rockdraw.py <<'EOF'


def draw_bedded(z, cell, rock, tone, bed_m=6.0, pitch=4.0, light_rc=(-0.38, -0.92), seed=0, **kw):
    """Horizontally bedded rock (Columbia River basalt; Imhof Fig.165, p.240, and Fig.178-9): the rock
    wall is a stack of ledges. Each bed contour becomes a ledge: a dark line (the overhang shadow) with a
    lit lip on its upslope side; between ledges short fall-line strokes (the columns / joints), dense on
    shaded faces, sparse on lit ones. Returns the same ink classes as draw()."""
    import strokes as S
    H, W = z.shape
    rng = np.random.default_rng(seed)
    ink, g = draw(z, cell, rock, tone, pitch=pitch, light_rc=light_rc, seed=seed, row_m=bed_m, **kw)
    ink[ink == 4] = 2                                # gullies are notches here, not long dark lines
    zs = ndi.gaussian_filter(z, 0.8)
    for lev, poly in S.contour_lines(zs, bed_m, offset=rng.uniform(0, bed_m)):
        pts = S.resample(poly, 0.6)
        for r, c in pts:
            ri, ci = int(round(r)), int(round(c))
            if 0 <= ri < H - 1 and 0 <= ci < W and rock[ri, ci] and rng.random() < 0.93:
                ink[ri, ci] = 3                        # the ledge shadow
                # lip: the upslope neighbour is lit
                gy, gx = np.gradient(zs[max(ri - 1, 0):ri + 2, max(ci - 1, 0):ci + 2])
                up = (int(np.sign(gy.mean())), int(np.sign(gx.mean())))
                q = (ri + up[0], ci + up[1])
                if 0 <= q[0] < H and 0 <= q[1] < W and rock[q] and ink[q] != 3:
                    ink[q] = 5
    return ink, g
EOF

"""Before | after: the place lanes' current renders beside the Imhof method on the same place (iso)."""
import numpy as np, sheet as SH
from PIL import Image
W = '~/work/voxsim-wt/'
PAIRS = [
    ('enchant_aasgard', W + 'place-enchant/renders/enchant/i2/u_region_iso.png', 'Enchantments (Aasgard, Dragontail NE face)'),
    ('sky_mtindex', W + 'place-skykomish/renders/sky/i4u/i4_mt_index_far.png', 'Skykomish (Mount Index)'),
    ('yakima_reds', W + 'place-yakima/renders/y/p46/y2_hero_cliffband_eve.png', 'Yakima Canyon (Red\'s, basalt rimrock)'),
]
rows = []
for name, lane, lab in PAIRS:
    iso = np.load(f'../refs/{name}_iso2.npy')
    rows.append([(SH.fit(Image.open(lane), h=300, smooth=True), 'BEFORE: place lane render (its own framing) - ' + lab),
                 (SH.fit(iso, h=300), 'AFTER: Imhof method, 2 m/px map draped at 30 deg iso')])
SH.sheet(rows, 'The place lanes now | the Imhof method on the same lidar',
         'Framings differ (the lane cameras are not reproduced); compare how rock, light and large forms are stated.',
         out='../sheets/lanes_before_after.png')
EOF
echo ok

Agent: Cold critic of place tests

Meanwhile, update LESSONS with the copies' critic verdicts and section 3.

Read: books/imhof/LESSONS.md

Now rewrite sections 2.3–3 to reflect what actually happened, including the critic.

Bash: cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; python3 - <<'EOF' p='LESSONS.md'; s=open(p).read() a=s.index("### 2.3 Master copies"); b=s.index("## 4. What did not carry") new='''### 2.3 
cd ~/work/pixelart-studies/books/imhof; python3 - <<'EOF'
p='LESSONS.md'; s=open(p).read()
a=s.index("### 2.3 Master copies"); b=s.index("## 4. What did not carry")
new='''### 2.3 Master copies of the rock drawing (sheets `copy_178_12.png`, `copy_178_15.png`, `copy_161.png`, `copy_165_r4.png`, `copy_165_r5.png`)

I copied four of his drawings at pixel size, placing the structure by hand from a gridded plate
(`code/grid.py`), and then asked a fresh critic to judge them cold against the plates. The critic's
ranking was 161 > 178-15 > (the lidar test) > 178-12 > 165, and its main charge ran through all of them:
**everything evenly spaced, the same width, axis-aligned; Imhof's economy is uneven, dense where the
form turns, empty where it is lit.** That is the most useful sentence anyone said about this work.

**Fig.178-12, steep scale-like buttresses** ("ridge crests are not drawn in"). Copy 1 put feathered barbs
on hand-drawn spines: combs. Copy 2 did what Imhof tells students to do (p.273): only the gully lines
by hand, a surface implied by them, strokes down its fall line at constant pitch weighted by its light.
The strokes end against each other where they converge into a gully and no crest is drawn. Measured on
the plate: stroke pitch 13 source px = 2.2-2.9 px at copy size, median stroke 5 px, ink share 0.29
(mine 0.35). **The critic's verdict holds, and it's still partial:** the strokes meet the gullies from
below as barbs, so it reads as herringbone; in the plate they arrive at an acute angle from above and
fan with each buttress. I tilted the surface and shortened the dark gully floors, and it's better but
not right. The plate's structure (which way the scales face) is the thing I still haven't read correctly.

**Fig.178-15, a slab wall channelled by gullies** ("a light medium tone of horizontal form lines; each
gully a crisp illuminated side paralleled by a well defined shadow"). The form lines are the contours of
the implied surface, broken into dashes at his pitch (~4 px); each gully is a 2-3 px shadow wedge with
the pixel on its light side scraped clean. After the critic said the form lines crossed the gullies dead
straight, I deepened the cuts until the lines hook down into every gully (chevrons). Still wrong: the
gully lines are ruler-straight and the dashes too regular. The mark itself is the most transferable in
the book: **a gully = dark line + one lit pixel beside it, never a dark line alone** (178-17 is the
failure).

**Fig.161, granite** (masses and needles). A pen drawing doesn't copy into pixels stroke for stroke:
reduced photographically (his own Fig.192 failure) it's a tangle, and transferred stroke by stroke it's
graffiti. It carries when translated into **values**: masses read off the pen's density (lit fronts,
right flanks in shade), his long flutes kept, one step darker on lit faces and a crevice step in shade,
each crevice with a lit pixel on its light side, contrast falling toward the foot (p.263), a garlanded
foot where snow tongues reach up the gullies (p.247). The critic called it the only copy that works as
pixel art, and named its faults: the shadow flutes are a picket fence of equal bands (his converge,
split and taper), the skyline has gone soft (his gendarmes and notches are smooth cones in mine), and the
flat outlined foreground ridge is another style.

**Fig.165, horizontally bedded rock** (the Yakima case). Five rounds, and the weakest copy. (1) A band chart
with barcode joints. (2) His strokes transferred one-to-one: pen graffiti. (3) A tidier band chart.
(4) Looking at the plate at 11 screen px per pixel showed the lower wall is **rounded pillars with
tombstone tops**, and the "trees" are gullies branching up into the bench. The critic: it now reads as a
fence, the horizontal bedding (the subject!) had become vertical pillars, and the gully "trees" read as
posts. (5) I put the ledge lines back across the pillars and turned the trunks into recesses; better, but
the vertical rhythm still dominates. The honest lesson: **I kept drawing the joints I could see, and lost
the beds that are the structure.** Imhof's text says it outright (Fig.165: "banded, stepped slopes").

**What the copies taught, which I couldn't have got from the text:**

1. In the Swiss style the darkness of rock comes from the density of strokes on the shaded faces; the
   ground under them is lighter than you expect.
2. Pitch: 3 px is the finest that reads, 4 px for form lines; at 2 px strokes merge into tone.
3. Only the skeleton (gully lines) is placed by hand; everything else can follow from it.
4. A pen drawing is translated through its values; the line itself doesn't survive reduction.
5. Uneven economy is the style. Anything evenly spaced reads as a machine.

### 2.4 Rock at region scale on real lidar (sheet `rock_1m_aasgard.png`)

`code/rockdraw.py`: evenly spaced fall-line streamlines (Jobard-Lefer) on the rock mask, pitch 4 px; on
shaded faces long strokes, the dark ink where the face is darkest; on lit faces a two-pixel tick under the
upper edge or nothing ("an extremely light distribution of fine hachures", p.262); gully lines from flow
accumulation × concavity drawn first, each with a scraped lit strip; no crest lines. `row_m` breaks the
strokes into Lehmann's rows (p.214), as on the LK25.

Three things I got wrong on the way, all visible in the sheets' history:

- **Pitch 3 with 2-px heavy strokes read as "=" bars.** On a face whose fall line runs nearly E-W in plan
  (Dragontail's NE face, measured: downhill vector (row −0.30, col +0.68)) the second pixel stacks under
  the first. The strokes had to stay 1 px wide; weight is carried by length and by the darker ink.
- **Strokes outside the rock** (gully lines on meadow) read as hair. Mask them to rock (critic).
- **Light ground vs large forms.** Strokes on a pure light ground look most like the LK25 at 1 m/px, but on
  a whole rock mountain (Mount Index) they erase the big dark flank: Imhof's large-form rule loses to his
  rock-hachure rule. Strokes over the full-range tone turned to mud (dark on dark). What works is the
  tone **compressed** to steps ~2.5-5 under the rock, strokes 2-3 steps below it: the big flank stays dark
  and the strokes still read. This is his p.340 combination ("shaded skeletal outlines or rock hachures
  with corresponding shading tones"), found by failing both ways.

## 3. Tests where it matters (sheets `place_*.png`, `lanes_before_after.png`)

`code/run_places.py` runs `place.paint_plan` on four 1024 m windows of the voxsim place packs (1 m lidar,
canopy height, water), at 1 and 2 m/px, and drapes the 2 m map on the lidar at 30° iso.

- **Enchantments, Aasgard / Dragontail NE face.** At 2 m/px the plan reads as a Swiss relief map: one dark
  NE flank carved by feathered gullies, lit slabs above, the glacier and tarns flat. At 1 m/px the face is a
  field of fall-line strokes gathered into gullies. Weak: the gentle granite ground between (camouflage
  blotches of two tone steps plus the slab form lines), which in the photo is a mottle of snow and slab.
- **Skykomish, Mount Index.** The strongest test: the NE walls are one dark stroked mass, the SW slopes lit
  meadow and rock; the 30° iso gives a convincing peak with rock bands on its flanks. Weak: the meadow is
  one flat yellow-green; the real mountain is darker and more wooded (the canopy layer finds only 4.5 %
  trees there).
- **Yakima Canyon, Red's.** Steppe colours and relief read; the basalt rimrock uses the bedded variant
  (`rockdraw.draw_bedded`: 9 m ledge lines with overhang shadows, lit lips, short column strokes). It reads
  as dark stepped rock at 1 m/px, but at 2 m/px and in iso the rimrock is too small to state the canyon's
  banding; the lane's render states it more strongly with plain black bands. Fig.165 is the right model and
  I haven't carried it well yet (see the copy).
- **Skykomish, Index (valley).** Forest dominates (71 % canopy); forest carries the relief shading steps with
  lit crowns, as Imhof requires (p.332); the Town Wall granite reads as a stroked wall.

Before | after against the lane renders is only roughly comparable (different cameras), but the difference
in how rock and big forms are stated is clear: the Enchantments lane render is a flat-lit white field;
the method gives the peaks a lit and a shaded side and draws the walls.

'''
s=s[:a]+new+s[b:]
open(p,'w').write(s)
EOF
echo ok

A fresh critic judged the four place sheets and the before|after sheet against the orthos. Its verdict,
which I accept:

- **Large forms and light: right, and better than the lanes** for Enchantments (the lane render has "no
  light direction and no forms") and Mount Index (the lane shows "a forest carpet and no mountain"). The
  Enchantments iso was its strongest frame.

  dark bands say "rimrock canyon". Relief is anaemic in the steppe and at Index (the valley window reads as a
  posterized hillshade: weakest sheet).
- Missing content: Mount Index's snowfields and tarns (my snow mask exists only for the Enchantments), the
  Index gravel bars and town; the rock mask comes from slope alone, and on Mount Index it may be following the
  shadow side more than the real rock.

## 4. What did not carry, and why""")
s+='''
## 6. Where it stands, and what I'd do next

**Carried faithfully:** the tone model (flat = mid step, diffuse WNW light, local adjustment, large-form
emphasis, aerial perspective by elevation, Leonardo's transitions as dither seams); the skeleton-first order
and the gully mark (dark line + lit pixel); fall-line strokes gathered into gullies at a legible pitch; the
compressed tone under rock strokes; forest carrying the shading; the colour recipe (warm lights, hazy
blue-grey shade). The Mount Index and Aasgard plans at 2 m/px read as Swiss relief maps in pixels.

**Not yet:** the rock drawing is still more texture than drawing. In order:

1. **Crests as a hard light/shadow break** (p.170 rule h, p.262): per-facet tone decided from the landform
   on each side of the crest line, so the change happens across one pixel.
2. **Uneven economy** (critic, twice): stroke length and density from the local form (long in gullies, short
   ticks on ribs, none on lit slabs), not one pitch everywhere.
3. **Bedded rock by beds, not joints** (Fig.165): Yakima needs the canyon wall stated as 3-4 dark bands with
   lit ledges at region scale before any column strokes; the lane's black bands are closer to Imhof than my
   stitches.
4. **Rock mask from geology + ortho**, not slope alone; snow masks for every place.
5. Copies: redo 178-12 until the scales face the right way, and 161's flutes as tapering, joining couloirs
   with a hand-drawn gendarme skyline.

**Resources.** Everything ran on one core, `nice -n 10`: a 1024 m window at 1 and 2 m/px plus the iso takes
2-4 minutes (pure-Python streamlines). The study folder is ~200 MB (scanned pages 57 MB, OCR, DEM windows,
swisstopo references).
'''
